Why the Falklands Is a Pressure Point For the U.K.—and How Milei, Trump Are Stoking Tensions
2026-09-04

Why the Falklands Is a Pressure Point For the U.K.—and How Milei, Trump Are Stoking Tensions

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Javier Milei, Argentina's President, speaks at the Buenos Aires Stock Exchange in Argentina on July 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Argentina’s President Javier Milei has escalated his country’s claim over the Falkland Islands, a British overseas territory, warning he will place economic pressure by sanctioning oil companies with operations in the area. The Falkland Islands are "Argentine, by history and by right, there is no debate about it,” he said in a nationally televised address Thursday evening. Milei argued the “winds of change blowing around the world” are favorable to Argentina’s long-claimed sovereignty over the South Atlantic archipelago, citing remarks made by his ally, U.S. President Donald Trump, who recently suggested the U.S. would be reviewing its neutrality on the dispute. The Argentine President flagged a planned oil project in the area as a cause for concern. Within the next 18 months, two companies—one British and the other Israeli—are set to start extractions from the Sea Lion oilfield, which lies north of the islands and holds an estimated 1.7 billion barrels of oil. Milei described the project as an urgent threat to Argentina’s sovereignty. "If we do not act swiftly, in a few months they will have the material capacity to access the oil that lies underneath our sea—something that has never happened before,” he said. The Falkland Islands, known in Argentina as “Islas Malvinas,” sit roughly 300 miles from the Argentine coast, and have been a British territory for almost two centuries, but Argentina has never accepted British sovereignty over the area. Vowing to take action, Milei said he plans to send a bill to Congress to strengthen Argentina’s response to what he views as attacks on its sovereignty. Responding to the economic threats, the U.K. government reasserted its “unshakeable” commitment to the archipelago on Friday, with British Defense Secretary Wes Streeting insisting: “Milei’s statement overnight tells us more about domestic politics in Argentina than it does about the Falkland Islands.” “The U.K. supports the right of the Falkland Islanders to develop their natural resources for their own economic benefit, and this is an integral part of their right to self-determination,” a spokesperson for Prime Minister Andy Burnham told TIME. “We continue to have a diplomatic relationship with Argentina, and from our perspective we want to continue to strengthen that.” Here’s what to know about the contentious history of the Falkland Islands and why Milei and Trump’s remarks have sparked concern amid mounting geopolitical tensions. The Falklands' defense and foreign policy is handled by the U.K., but otherwise it has an internal government of its own. The territory has its own constitution and as of 1983, residents of the islands have been British citizens. In a 2013 referendum, Falkland residents voted overwhelmingly in favor of remaining under British rule. The referendum saw 92% of all eligible voters turn out, with 99.8% of votes being “yes.” “The Falklands are British because Falkland Islanders choose to be British,” Streeting said Friday, pointing to the will of the voters. The first recorded landing on the island was made in 1690 by English naval captain John Strong. Britain took possession of West Falkland in 1765. France and Spain each had settlements on the islands at different times, but Britain re-established control in 1833 and has since administered the islands. “Argentina claims the U.K. took the islands as an act of imperialism in the 1830s, but the remedy to colonial acquisition would be self-determination,” says Marc Weller, programme director of the international law programme at U.K.-based think tank Chatham House. Given the British government’s insistence on Falklanders deciding their future, “it is therefore acting in accordance with the principle of self-determination,” he tells TIME. Under the rule of military dictator Leopoldo Galtieri, Argentine forces attempted to stake a territorial claim in April 1982, sparking a 74-day war that Britain won. Argentina has since continued to claim sovereignty over the islands and the rhetoric has become a unifying issue in the country, experts tell TIME. The issue recently reared its head during Argentina’s victory over England in the semi-finals of the 2026 FIFA World Cup, with some Argentine players holding up a banner reading "Las Malvinas son Argentinas,” which translates to "The Falklands are Argentine.” “The Argentinian nation is easily united behind this claim through the Falkland Islands. It's a rallying point, and it's perhaps no accident that as the present Argentinean government is in a little bit of trouble, that they have rediscovered the issue,” says Weller, pointing towards Milei’s controversial fiscal policies and falling popularity among the public. Argentina's soccer players celebrate a World Cup victory over England with a banner reading "The Falklands are Argentine" on July 15, 2026. Sebastian Frej—Getty Images Milei has previously ruled out using force to claim the islands, instead pushing for a diplomatic resolution. Still, his newly-announced plans to impose sanctions against oil companies with operations in the area pose an economic threat. The two petroleum firms heading the Sea Lion oil drilling project have already felt the impact of Milei’s proposed sanctions. British company Rockhopper Exploration’s stock value fell by around 10% on Friday morning, with Israeli firm Navitas Petroleum stock value falling by at least 4.5%. “It's an attempt to add some uncertainty, perhaps to inhibit investment,” suggests Weller, adding he’s doubtful any such threats will impact Downing Street’s position on the sovereignty dispute. Argentine President Javier Milei alongside U.S. President Donald Trump at the World Economic Forum in Davos, Switzerland, on Jan. 22, 2026. Fabrice Coffrini—Getty Images The dispute over sovereignty remains a tense geopolitical issue. China, for example, has been vocal in its support of Argentina’s claim, encouraging negotiations to take place on the matter. The U.S., meanwhile, has historically recognized the U.K.’s “administration of the islands,” but has maintained a neutral position regarding sovereignty. However, Trump has indicated that the U.S. may be open to reversing that position. The Falklands sovereignty became a talking point in April as the U.S. and Europe drifted on account of Trump lambasting European leaders for refusing to get actively involved in the Iran war. A leaked Pentagon email, which reportedly suggested various ways the Administration could punish NATO allies it believes let the U.S. down, suggested a review of the U.S. position on Britain’s claim to the Falkland Islands, according to Reuters. Earlier this week, when asked if the U.S. position on the islands was under review, Trump told reporters in the Oval Office: “I always review every position. That's just one of many." In an interview with a British broadcast channel that aired on Thursday, Trump did not directly respond when asked if the U.S. would come to the U.K’s aid if Argentina claimed the Falklands. Trump instead pivoted to discuss the Iran war, repeating his claims that the U.K. let him down. “Your country was not there to help me,” he insisted. When approached by TIME, a spokesperson for Burnham said such comments “are a matter for the U.S. President.” TIME has reached out to the White House for comment. Andrew Gawthorpe, a lecturer at Leiden University and an expert in the foreign policy of the U.S., tells TIME a Washington reversal on the issue “would be an enormous defeat for the U.K. diplomatically and a huge blow” to the special U.K.-U.S. relationship. Weller agrees, adding that if any U.S. policy were to go against the territorial integrity of the U.K., then “Britain would have to respond quite strongly.” Trump’s remarks on the Falklands this week could prove difficult for Burnham, who inherited a splintered Washington-London relationship upon replacing Keir Starmer as Prime Minister in July. “On the one hand, Burnham needs to appear like a strong leader,” says Gawthorpe. “But on the other hand, I think he's very aware that he needs to not annoy Trump any more than is strictly necessary.”

Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump announced on Thursday that Adam Telle, the Assistant Secretary of the Army for Civil Works, will temporarily replace Dan Driscoll as the Army’s top civilian leader following Driscoll’s abrupt resignation from the role earlier this week. Trump lauded Telle as "a Great Patriot, who is respected by all” in a Truth Social post, saying Telle would take over as acting Army Secretary “effective immediately.” The news comes after Driscoll, who Trump nominated as Army Secretary last year after returning to the White House, stepped down amid reports of tension between him and Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. Hegseth praised Trump’s announcement on Thursday, calling Telle “strong and whip smart.” “Huge congrats to Adam,” Hegseth said in a post on X. “He is indeed a great Patriot — and will be an immediate asset to the @USArmy.” Telle was sworn into his current role as the Assistant Secretary of the Army for Civil Works last August, becoming the second person nominated by the President to an Army position in Trump's second term to be confirmed by the Senate, according to Telle's biography on the U.S. military’s website. In that position, Telle has served as a civilian official in the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers, an agency that builds and maintains infrastructure, including dams. According to his bio, he has had “principal authority to establish policy direction and to provide supervision of all aspects” of the civil works tasks that the agency performs for the Army, including projects aimed at reducing flood and storm damage. Before being appointed to his current role in the Trump Administration, Telle served as the chief of staff to Republican Sen. Bill Hagerty of Tennessee from 2021-2025, according to Legistorm. He previously worked under another Republican, then-Sen. Thad Cochran of Mississippi, for about a decade from 2007-2017, and held various roles in the office, including deputy chief of staff and legislative director, according to Legistorm. While serving in Cochran’s office, he helped with the lawmaker’s work on Hurricane Katrina recovery, according to his Army bio. Telle also served on the Senate Appropriations Committee’s subcommittee that was responsible for overseeing the Department of Homeland Security’s budget, according to his bio. During Trump’s first term in the Oval Office, Telle served on the White House Office of Legislative Affairs’ Senate team and “was President Trump’s chief liaison to the Senate,” according to his Army bio. Telle will step into his new role in the Army at a time of turmoil both abroad and within the Department of Defense. The war in Iran, which began on Feb. 28, has now stretched on for longer than six months and has sparked backlash from many Americans. And Telle will also be taking on the new position amid upheaval within the U.S. military’s senior ranks. About 20 military and civilian leaders have been ousted or have resigned since Hegseth took over the Department of Defense, including Driscoll earlier this week. Even some Republicans have criticized Hegseth for the chaos at the agency, including Sen. Thom Tillis of North Carolina, who has called on Trump to replace the Defense Secretary. “[Hegseth] is creating a leadership void at the top of our military ranks,” Tillis said in a social media post this week. “I urge the President to find a new leader at the Pentagon who will retain and empower our military talent rather than diminish it.”

Rooney and Kate Mara Are Brilliant in Werner Herzog's Fractured Fairytale Bucking Fastard
2026-09-04

Rooney and Kate Mara Are Brilliant in Werner Herzog's Fractured Fairytale Bucking Fastard

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Rooney Mara and Kate Mara as Joan and Jean Holbrooke Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Almost nothing in Werner Herzog’s fractured fairytale Bucking Fastards, playing in competition here at the Venice Film Festival, makes literal sense. But it all makes a kind of sideways sense, especially if, before watching, you can will yourself into a Herzogian brainspace, a place where possibly nonexistent elephants roam the Earth, where ancient cave drawings bear witness to the enduring creativity and wonder of humankind, where mad dreamers insist it’s possible to haul a multi-ton ship over a mountain. In that context, two inseparable sisters who speak only in unison, who are crazily lovesick over the man who has spurned them, and who believe they can dig a literal tunnel into a land of freedom, acceptance, and love: in a world where our deepest desires are so seldom met, how nuts does that really sound? Real-life sisters Rooney Mara and Kate Mara play Joan and Jean Holbrooke, sisters living in Ireland—they are not, they adamantly insist, twins—who share every emotion, every thought, every waking moment as if they were a single entity. They dress exactly alike, in droopy trenchcoats and modest granny-style skirts; they both wear their long, silky hair parted neatly in the middle and tied partially back, like identical waterfalls. They don’t finish each other’s sentences; instead, they form their thoughts and words in unison and speak as one. It’s an eerie effect the first time you hear it. Even more unsettling, it comes to sound completely normal as the movie wears on. Joan and Jean have fallen for a single man, their handsome rapscallion neighbor Gareth Mulroney (a saucy, salacious Orlando Bloom). It’s love at first sight—or is that sights?—when he bursts into their local church, a tankard of beer in hand, stirring up nothing but trouble. Afterward, they breathlessly wait for him to emerge through the church doors. Their eyes gleaming like double constellations, they proposition him: “I’d love to show you my inner cowgirl!” they tell him, the words tumbling out more like a song than a sentence. He’s charmed, intrigued; he’s horny. The trio land in bed, with Gareth in the middle flanked by his semi-nude cutie cowgirls, who ooh and ahh over him adoringly. They’re Goldilocks and he’s their just-right. This is how a sex scene, as imagined by Herzog, plays out. But their bliss is short-lived. That meet-cute and its aftermath are relayed in flashback as the sisters huddle together in a courtroom, essentially defending themselves against charges of stalking. Gareth, after dallying with Joan and Jean for a time, has thrown them over and taken a wife. They can’t believe it—in their futile rage, they set the interior of his car on fire. (Their procurement of the gasoline they use to do so is a cracked mini-adventure by itself.) They also set the postbox outside his house aflame. Before the bewigged judge, Gareth’s lawyer tries to argue that the relationship was purely sexual. Joan and Jean justify their actions plaintively. “It was love!” they cry in unison, and later, “I am never going to stop loving him!” Joan and Jean, forever in sync Courtesy of Courtesy of Gateway to Orkney LLC But there’s no hope. Their linked hearts are broken, and the court case has brought them both fame and embarrassment. A kind villager (John Kavanagh) takes pity on them, knowing that the best thing for them is to get away. He offers them the use of a flat he doesn’t need, located in another town. He urges them not to open the door to strangers, people who want only to take advantage of them. From there, Bucking Fastard becomes a set of wriggly vignettes more than a linear story. There’s a sleazy neighbor (Malcolm Adams) who attempts to take, and post on the Internet, pictures of them undressing. (They put their heads together and cook up a giggly-devious plan to outwit him.) A kind social worker, played by Domhnall Gleeson, tries to show them how to collaborate in the task of opening a tin of sardines. But soon even their cozy flat comes to feel like a prison: they strike out on an adventure, going to live with an aunt who loves them (Marion O’Dwyer) and who encourages them to explore a mysterious cave near her country property, one whose twists and turns ignite their two-for-one imaginations. A fox named Cupid, with a perpetually alarmed-looking face, becomes their mascot. Through it all, they continue to mourn their lost love. But perhaps they can find a new one? What does any of this mean? Though the story was inspired by real-life twins from York, England, Freda and Greta Chaplin—who spoke in unison and, like Jean and Joan, did everything together—the meandering storyline is pure, sweet, gaga Herzog. The picture’s most radiant sequences take place in the girls' beloved cave, where they toil for years to dig their way, admittedly nonsensically, to a better life. Herzog and his frequent cinematographer Peter Zeitlinger film the two of them, within a halo of light as they dutifully chip through a rocky passageway—their twin images are as luminous as a delicately carved shell cameo. The Mara sisters are brilliant here, their eyes as hungry and haunted as those of an orphan in a Keane painting. You laugh with them, not at them, when they take revenge on that peeping tom. But there’s also something deeply touching about their refusal to give up on love. There’s a metaphorical idea shimmering beneath the surface of Bucking Fastard: it’s as if, through this strange and somewhat simple story, Herzog were trying to explore the things women want but don’t dare demand. Here, those longings are voiced in stereo. And yes, men can sometimes drive you crazy. What can you do? The ending of Bucking Fastard might be considered a flaw; it’s hard to know exactly what has happened, or what it means. But then, an ambiguous ending is one we’re free to write ourselves. How can you not want the best for these dreamy, heartbroken girls, who at one point race through their cave in matching gauzy white gowns, like freedom-loving butterflies? You’ll want to believe that their hearts have at last found a home—a cave not of forgotten dreams, but of remembered ones, made sweeter with every recounting.
The Atlantic
Why Wendell Berry Didn’t Worry About the Future
2026-09-05

Why Wendell Berry Didn’t Worry About the Future

The Atlantic | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Wendell Berry was not an easy man to reach. When I wanted to interview him for this magazine in the spring of 2015, his publicist warned me that a press request would take some doing. Berry didn’t have a cellphone. He didn’t use email. He didn’t even have a computer. He wrote most of his drafts by hand during the day, without electric light. And he definitely didn’t make himself available to any old reporter who wanted to talk. I knew this already, of course. Berry, who died on Monday at the age of 92, lived a life defined by acts of radical abstention. He said no emphatically and often; principled refusal was a central theme of his writing and the lore that surrounded him. The publicist spent the better part of a week trying to reach Berry on my behalf. This involved multiple phone calls to his farmhouse, in Henry County, Kentucky, where Berry and his wife, Tanya, had lived since the mid-1960s. Wendell spent most of his time outdoors, the publicist explained, so he often simply wasn’t around to hear the landline ring. After a while, though, she told me that she and Berry had managed to connect, and that he would consider my request. He asked that I write him a letter about the article I had in mind, which I emailed to the publicist so she could print it out and send it to him by post. From the February 1991 issue: Out of your car, off your horse A few weeks later, I received a voicemail from a number I didn’t recognize. “Hello, Mr. Fassler,” said a voice that was deep and hoarse with age. “This is Wendell Berry.” A giant of Luddism was speaking out of my iPhone. He asked me to call him back. When we spoke, he told me in a warm, genial way that I was out of luck. “I’m just so covered up with work,” he said, “and I can’t tell if it’s because I’m just busy or because I’m old.” But he’d thought a bit about my letter. I’d asked him to take part in “By Heart,” a series in which authors discussed the passages from literature that had most shaped their writing and outlook. He said that his forthcoming book, Our Only World, contained a section that the magazine might adapt in some way. It hinged on some verses from Jesus’s Sermon on the Mount, as described in the Gospel of Matthew: “Take no thought for the morrow: for the morrow shall take thought for the things of itself. Sufficient unto the day is the evil thereof.” He asked me to read that section of his book, and to call him back once I had so that we could talk. So I did. And I’ve been thinking about its message ever since. I was surprised, at first, that Berry felt so drawn to this specific moment from the Bible. Jesus’s instruction to not worry about the future seemed at odds with much of what Berry had written. Many of the social and environmental problems that Berry railed against were really problems of short-term thinking, including the pursuit of quick profit, the extractive poisoning of the land, and the love of cheap thrills over deeper, more hard-won pleasures. “Think not of tomorrow” seemed to better describe the ethos of Berry’s longtime nemeses: coal-industry strip miners, big agribusiness, the global financial system. It was also an odd choice for someone who seemed so concerned about the excesses of modern life. Berry struck me as someone who absolutely would fixate on the general worsening direction of things. Fretting about the future is a favorite preoccupation of the conscientious. Berry occasionally admitted to his own worried state of mind on the page, including in one of his best-known poems: “When despair for the world grows in me,” he wrote, “I wake in the night at the least sound / in fear of what my life and my children’s lives may be.” Nevertheless, in the part of his book that he asked me to read, he wrote, “Taking thought for the morrow is, fairly predictably, a waste of time.” He insisted that what we call “the future” is a flimsy and insubstantial thing at best—a phantom we obsess over that never really arrives, or never arrives in the way we imagine. “Most of the bad possibilities I have worried about have never happened,” Berry wrote. “By taking thought for the morrow we have invested, and wasted, a lot of effort in preparing for morrows that never came.” Obsessing about the future, he argued, also has devastating moral consequences. It allows people to avoid engaging with the urgent realities of the present. I don’t know whether Berry, given his extremely offline lifestyle, ever learned about doomscrolling—the way social media can serve as a collective orgy of dread. But he’s right that, when people become too obsessed with a future that may or may not befall them, they tend to turn their back on the moment they’re in. “The time is coming when it will be too late to save the world. Oh, oh, oh,” he wrote, lampooning the handwringer in so many of us. “If that is the way our minds are afflicted, we and our world are dead already. The present is going by and we are not in it.” As I see it, this was the core purpose of Berry’s work: He dared his readers to live in a way that honors the situation at hand, at this very second. That mandate suffused everything he wrote about. He exhorted readers to enjoy the food on their plates, to bear witness to the places that surround them, and to revel in shared experience with the people in their orbit. Honoring the present was a joyful thing for Wendell Berry. It was also a form of rewarding but very difficult work. It meant slow, careful deliberation about the things he was willing to let into his life, whether that was a new piece of technology or a request from a journalist. And it meant searching for ways to disentangle himself, by turns and sometimes painfully, from destructive systems. The moral goal of a human being, he often argued, should be to do less harm right now. When one of his most famous essays, 1987’s “Why I Am Not Going to Buy a Computer,” resulted in astonished and outraged letters from readers, Berry replied by writing, “I did not say that I proposed to end forthwith all my involvement in harmful technology, for I do not know how to do that. I said merely that I want to limit such involvement, and to a certain extent I do know how to do that.” This response boils down to a simple, radical worldview: We must seek to understand the harms caused by the systems we’re implicated in, then strive to become less reliant on them. From the February 1992 issue: A jonquil for Mary Penn Berry was no purist. He understood that no 20th- or 21st-century American can live a wholly blameless life. He lamented needing to drive a truck, the unavoidable, occasional use of gas-powered equipment on his farm. He knew that his ancestors had enslaved their fellow human beings and that he, in direct and indirect ways, had benefited from that obscene bondage. “I am owned by the blood of all of them,” he wrote in an early poem, “My Great-Grandfather’s Slaves.” “We cannot be free of each other.” Moral blamelessness isn’t possible. But Berry’s larger point was that people should strive for something better, here and now. As he put it in Our Only World, with his trademark aphoristic brilliance: “Maybe we could give up saving the world and start to live savingly in it.” This reframes Jesus’s “sufficient unto the day is the evil thereof” as a simple, clarifying injunction: Forget the future, and work toward what is good today. I did speak with Berry a second time. We talked a little while about the Sermon on the Mount, but I didn’t get him to agree to a formal interview. I could tell he wanted to help me, to be a sport. Still, I sensed something else nagging at him: the irresistible presence of the now. He was here, living on this Earth. He had land to tend to, animals to care for, a wife to love, books to write—more than 50 of them, by the end. Maybe someday, in some dimly glimpsed future, he would find some more time to talk with me. But right then, at that moment, there was just too much to do.

J. D. Vance’s Disingenuous Defense of Tucker Carlson
2026-09-05

J. D. Vance’s Disingenuous Defense of Tucker Carlson

The Atlantic | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
When J. D. Vance was asked at a press conference on Thursday about his relationship with Tucker Carlson, the media personality and ultra-right-wing conspiracy theorist, he displayed a surprising side of himself: human, vulnerable, almost touchingly naive. “Tucker is a friend, despite the fact that we disagree on a number of things,” the vice president said. “And I’m not going to play the game where I throw friends under the bus because I have political disagreements with them. I think that is a terrible place for the United States to go. And if you have a disagreement with somebody politically, even over a deeply held issue, you should still be friends with that person, because the whole republic depends on people of different perspectives being able to talk to one another.” This was a matter not of politics, Vance was at pains to emphasize, but of friendship. If Vance were to renounce Carlson over a matter as trivial as, say, Carlson’s oddly positive assessment of Adolf Hitler, what hope is there for society? The bonds of affection between Vance and Carlson are the very glue holding together the American experiment. And yet, tempting though it might be to imagine Vance clasping hands with the children of the world and teaching them to sing in perfect harmony, some notes of skepticism are warranted. Perhaps Vance’s affection for Carlson has more to do with holding the loyalty of his friend’s enormous audience. For one, Vance is not known for cordially disagreeing with people. He has accumulated an unusual number of bitter enemies by flinging insults at people—including Senate Democrats (“You’re full of shit, and everyone knows it”), reporters (“scumbags”), and “childless cat ladies.” Last month, Vance hurled himself into an obscure debate over burrito prices to make a gratuitous swipe at a conservative Washington Post columnist’s weight. At the very same press conference where he waxed so earnestly about open dialogue, Vance mocked the idea that he would talk to former President Biden at the 9/11 ceremony (“I’m really looking forward to hanging out with Joe Biden. I’m sure that we’ll have many, many intense intellectual conversations, and it’ll be great”) and dismissed the Michigan Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed as “a crazy person.” Vance’s dig at El-Sayed, and the context in which he made it, revealed a lot more about his reasons for maintaining the alliance with Carlson than did his encomium to friendship. Vance was trying to illustrate that he and Carlson do not agree on everything. Carlson had recently praised El-Sayed as “America First” and “literally Trumpian,” apparently calculating that the candidate’s anti-Zionism outweighs his many left-wing positions. That view was the one Vance chose to highlight as his point of disagreement, rather than several other controversial remarks Carlson has made. Examples include but are not limited to his endorsement of “Great Replacement” and other conspiracy theories, his expressions of sympathy for Russia and its war against Ukraine, and his sympathetic interviews with figures such as Nick Fuentes, Alex Jones, and the World War II–revisionist historian Darryl Cooper, who called Winston Churchill “the chief villain of the Second World War” and described much of the Holocaust as mistaken deaths resulting from poor planning. All of these statements have left Carlson teetering at the edge of respectability within the Republican Party. He has become one of the most prominent exponents of a strain of right-wing populism that flirts with and in some cases embraces isolationism, anti-Semitism, and general crackpottery. Vance has courted this faction of the right. He and Carlson have a partnership that has lasted nearly a decade and has yielded dozens of interviews. In the course of this partnership, they may well have formed a genuine friendship. But Vance has discarded plenty of friends as he has cycled through various political identities. The difference between Vance’s ex-friends and Carlson is that Carlson is still a useful ally in a Republican factional conflict. When Carlson upset Fox News in 2021 by endorsing the Great Replacement theory, which holds that Democrats are using illegal immigration to replace the current electorate and cement power, Vance wrote on social media, “Tucker Carlson is the only powerful figure who consistently challenges elite dogma—on both cultural and economic questions. That is why they try to destroy him. Don’t fall for it this time, or any other.” Vance went on to endorse the same theory in advance of the next midterm elections. “Democrat politicians who have decided that they can’t win reelection in 2022 unless they bring in a large number of new voters to replace the voters that are already here” are orchestrating lax border enforcement, he said on Carlson’s show that year. The Carlson-Vance partnership has generated more conservative blowback over the past few years as Carlson has progressed from skepticism of Israel into anti-Semitism, which Carlson denies. Traditional Republicans who have supported, or at least gone along with, the party’s attacks on Muslims and immigrants and its rejection of democratic norms have spoken out against cracking the door open for anti-Semites. Building an authoritarian, ultranationalist party that marinates in populist conspiracy theories but that abhors anti-Semitism turns out to be tricky. Helen Lewis: Carlson and Vance—two smart guys who play dumb for power Vance, heading into his expected 2028 presidential campaign, has a strong incentive to try to harness this right-wing energy. His political interests would be served if Republicans were to stop attacking Carlson and if Carlson were to tone down his criticism of the GOP. Hence, he seemed less concerned that Carlson had crossed a moral line than that, in the Michigan Senate race, he had crossed party lines. Carlson praising a member of the Democratic Party is more threatening to Vance than Carlson praising an apologist for the National Socialist German Workers’ Party. At this week’s press conference, the reporter asked Vance, “What do you say to those who are uneasy with your close relationship with Carlson and claim that you’re avoiding addressing a lot of these issues, which may be causing divisions within the GOP?” “What a travesty it is that in the United States of America, we let political disagreements define who our friends are going to be and not going to be,” Vance said. “So, yeah, Tucker is a friend of mine.” Suppose Carlson decided to support someone other than Vance for president in 2028. Does anybody think their friendship would survive?

The Rush-Job Presidency
2026-09-05

The Rush-Job Presidency

The Atlantic | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Construction is notoriously slow-going work. But in Donald Trump’s Washington, cranes and bulldozers appear like DoorDash deliveries: fast, frictionless, and on demand. To build the president’s White House ballroom, roughly 250 workers have been pouring concrete and installing steel framework, keeping the site humming for 20 hours a day, seven days a week. The National Park Service has proposed a similarly intensive schedule for Trump’s planned triumphal arch: Construction will begin, Interior Secretary Doug Burgum announced on Thursday, in the next two weeks. And officials are trying to shut down the Kennedy Center for two years to carry out renovations that consultants say will take twice as long if the building remains open during the work. These are very different and ambitious projects, but they share the same presumed deadline: January 20, 2029. They also share a risk: The compressed schedules could lead to shoddy work that will require more repairs later. Trump has less than three years to complete his transformation of Washington, D.C., before his projects fall into—and perhaps die in—the hands of a successor. “Nobody will do it once I’m gone. When I leave here, nobody will,” he told New York magazine. The next president, whomever they are, likely won’t share Trump’s enthusiasm for gold finishes and Italian marble. To get the work done, his administration has found ways to circumvent the procedural obstacles that tend to slow federal projects. Construction—one of the world’s least productive industries—may present its own paradox. Research has shown that long hours, additional shifts, and overlapping work tasks can shorten a project’s timeline—but only to a point. In many cases, the effort to build faster can invite new delays, higher costs, and revisions. Trump’s botched repairs to the Reflecting Pool ahead of the nation’s 250th-birthday celebrations, in July, offer a classic example of the problem. The ballroom project, a hulking party venue that overshadows the White House’s main building and its modest neoclassical design, could be another. The Supreme Court ruled 5–4 this week that its construction could continue, saying that the preservationists trying to halt the project did not have standing to sue. The three liberal dissenting justices—along with, notably, Chief Justice John G. Roberts—argued that the construction was “likely unlawful.” But government filings claim that it’s already 65 percent complete—even as project leaders have continually revised design plans. By the time a new lawsuit from a challenger with stronger standing advances through the courts, how far along will the ballroom be? Read: The Court’s ballroom decision is a sign of deeper problems “We have hundreds of happy construction workers in D.C. busily doing their job building the finest Ballroom/Military complex anywhere in the World,” Trump wrote on Truth Social this week after the Supreme Court decision. With his lawyers saying that the project will be “substantially” finished by November, the clock is ticking fast. Trump’s building spree presents a tricky calculation: The returns he wants are about time rather than cost. But speed in construction can get expensive fast, especially for a project whose anticipated costs have been hard to nail down. When Trump announced the ballroom plans in July 2025, he put the price at roughly $200 million, to be paid for by private donors. By October, he’d knocked down the East Wing of the White House without seeking any approvals. As the work has started, stopped (because of the preservationists’ lawsuit), and started again, the original estimate has tripled to $600 million, and taxpayers are expected to cover half of that amount, according to internal documents from the ballroom’s general contractor, Clark Construction, that were obtained by The Washington Post. Those projections could rise again. “Accelerated construction is always at higher cost, no matter what you do,” Awad Hanna, the chair of the construction-engineering and -management program at the University of Wisconsin at Madison, told me. There are essentially three ways to make construction move faster, according to Hanna: Workers can put in overtime, contractors can add second or third shifts, or the project can resort to overmanning—that is, adding an unusually large number of workers to a site. Each method may buy some time but sacrifices some productivity, Hanna said. Researchers have documented that trade-off for decades. A 1997 study published by the American Society of Civil Engineers examined 121 weeks of labor-productivity data from four industrial projects and found efficiency losses of roughly 10 to 15 percent for 50-to-60-hour workweeks. Hanna’s own research has found similar patterns. With overmanning, the problem is that there’s only so much room for specific tasks to be performed. As the site fills up with workers, they can find themselves competing for tools, materials, and information—or simply getting in one another’s way. Meanwhile, overtime can lead to fatigue for workers who are spending 10 to 12 hours on their feet, carrying heavy objects while also needing to remain diligent about their own safety. There is another way to speed up construction without adding hours or workers: starting construction before a design is complete. The industry calls this fast-tracking. But a 2023 study found that construction professionals perceived the fast-track strategy as posing a different set of risks, including construction errors and productivity declines, when better productivity is supposed to be the whole point. A certain amount of secrecy surrounds the White House worksite, in part because of a large military structure that is being built underground, beneath where the ballroom will stand. The methods to speed up the work are unclear, although the teams behind the project are reputable: Clark Construction is a seasoned federal contractor, and the architectural firm Shalom Baranes Associates repaired the Pentagon after 9/11. A White House spokesperson told me that the project is proceeding with the 20-hour schedule for now. In April, Trump turned his focus to the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, a century-old landmark that has long struggled with leaks and algae blooms. Trump first claimed that he could complete a repair project in a week for less than $2 million. “I have a guy who’s unbelievable at doing swimming pools,” he told reporters at the time. The costs climbed to more than $16 million, which included the installation of a waterproof liner tinted in a color that he called American-flag blue. But shortly after the pool reopened, in June, algae returned in force and the sealant peeled, leading crews to repeatedly drain and scrub the basin over the summer. Now it sits completely empty and fenced off. Trump accused vandals of damaging the pool, but his own U.S. attorney for D.C. refuted those claims, pointing to the contractor’s failures and the Interior Department providing “less than fulsome information at the outset of the case.” Federal officials recently hired a new contractor to deal with the algae. Trump has already cleared one obstacle blocking the ballroom. But the legal case is not over. Following the emergency ruling, the government still has to formally ask the Supreme Court to hear its appeal. And the National Trust for Historic Preservation, which brought the suit against Trump, can continue a fight in the lower courts. For each of Trump’s Washington projects, officials have beefed up their justifications in one way or another in the face of legal challenges. In the administration’s view: The Kennedy Center may have to be “taken down” if its planned two-year shutdown for renovations is blocked. (Critics have pushed back on officials’ claims that the building’s conditions are existentially dire.) The Reflecting Pool’s needed repairs were urgent enough to exempt them from the typical competitive-bidding process. (The urgency was the July 4 celebrations.) And now? The White House ballroom is needed for security reasons, because it’s connected to a military bunker. Read: The Kennedy Center has a problem that courts can’t fix The administration has also pointed to the ballroom’s progress in arguing against an injunction, raising a larger question about whether speed itself has become a form of power in Trump’s building spree. If a president can build faster than Congress or regulators can review, legal constraints may matter less in practice—not because they have disappeared but because enforcing them comes too late. This week’s Supreme Court ruling has already prompted at least one federal judge to consider its implications for another Trump project. Yesterday, U.S. District Judge Carl J. Nichols ordered lawyers representing the Interior Department to address how the Court’s ballroom decision affects its pending motion to dismiss a lawsuit challenging work on the Reflecting Pool. When asked about the start of excavations for the triumphal arch, the White House spokesperson said in an email, “Work will begin that does not require approval, but rather helps in the process. We will of course continue on our path of seeking final approval from all applicable entities.” The arch still needs final approval from the National Capital Planning Commission, to which Trump has added loyalists. But the administration has also been working to water down historic-preservation rules governing federal projects just as the arch has become subject to that process. Meanwhile, the Kennedy Center—which Trump also controls—cannot start its renovation until a judge signs off on the latest plan. But this week, the center removed a prominent artwork near the Potomac River, a sign that top-down change has begun. All three projects—the ballroom, the arch, and the Kennedy Center—may soon be moving forward simultaneously, red tape cleared (or ignored). But sped-up construction can impose its own obstacles. When unexpected delays crop up, even presidents have to wait.
The Atlantic
Don’t Ban AI on Campus
2026-09-05

Don’t Ban AI on Campus

The Atlantic | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Students returning to campus this semester are arriving with a great deal of anxiety about AI and what it will mean for their lives after college. They are right to be worried. New research from Stanford shows that, even though the much-hyped AI job apocalypse has yet to materialize, the technology has already caused a nearly 20 percent drop in employment for young workers in AI-exposed occupations, such as computer programming and customer-service work. Anxiety over the looming threat of AI may seem novel, but as a cognitive scientist who has long studied how fear affects learning and performance, I am all too familiar with this kind of unease. This is how I know it is also curable. Several years ago, my former graduate student Ian Lyons and I invited people who were anxious about math to have their brains scanned while they took a math test. We were surprised to find that it wasn’t the test itself that sent people’s brains into a panic. Rather, the neural signals associated with threat detection and pain were most acute when people were merely anticipating the test. We are all now living through AI’s waiting-for-the-math-test phase. AI is transforming classrooms, workplaces, and hiring needs—and experts say more turbulence is coming. Teachers and students are struggling to parse the difference between deploying AI effectively and exploiting the technology to cheat. But rather than tackling this disruptive change and the resulting anxiety head-on, many educators are trying to make AI simply go away. The University of Chicago’s social-science faculty have prohibited most technology and banned the use of AI tools from their core classes. Although law firms are beginning to embrace the benefits of generative AI, the technology apparently makes many law schools squeamish. In May, the UC Berkeley School of Law announced that students are prohibited from using AI in “conceptualizing, outlining, drafting, revising, translating, or editing any work submitted for credit.” The University of Chicago Law School has decided to ban all screens from first-year classrooms entirely. In an atmosphere of uncertainty—and given prevailing concerns about students using AI to cheat—such bans would seem to make some sense. But draconian mandates and screenless classes can’t be the only ways that colleges deal with AI. If universities hope to remain relevant in a world shaped by artificial intelligence, they should strive to guide students to understand and use these tools productively. In my research of math anxiety, we found that the best cure was practicing math. This points to a way forward. When we supply students with the space to thoughtfully explore and engage with these tools, we can help ensure that they graduate into an AI-enabled workforce with more confidence and competence than fear, and with an appreciation for how to use AI tools ethically. But if higher education opts for prohibition over practice, we will squander the chance to help shape how this technology gets used in the world—ensuring that the private sector will do this for us instead. Colleges must start by being clear about what a degree is actually worth in the AI age. At Dartmouth, the Ivy League university where I am president, we believe it’s our job to teach students how to do what AI can’t: decide which questions to ask, exercise sound judgment, navigate ethical complexity, reckon with uncertainty, and take responsibility for their decisions. Then, we have to give them the chance to practice these skills in concert with AI. Harnessing AI ethically and productively requires understanding what the technology is and where it falls short. This is why Dartmouth, along with other schools such as Johns Hopkins University and Rollins College, has employed a critical AI librarian, an educator who helps students and faculty interrogate these models, recognize their biases, and spot unsupported claims. At our DALI Lab, where students design and create technological products, they can now build and stress-test their own AI models, which elevates their understanding of the risks and rewards at play. Our ambition at Dartmouth is for every undergraduate to experience a team-based AI project that relates to their coursework by the time they graduate. Practice using AI matters even more when students start to specialize. To help prepare business students for AI use in the real world, Dartmouth’s Tuck School of Business now requires that they use the technology to build and test solutions for clients in their First-Year Project. Thanks to this experience, students who lacked AI expertise when they entered the program reported greater confidence using the technology by the end of their first year. At Dartmouth’s Geisel School of Medicine, our faculty developed an AI Patient Actor to help medical-school students sharpen their communication and interpersonal skills with the benefit of real-time feedback. The tool is now being used by more than 100 medical institutions worldwide. Walt Hunter: Bring back the blue-book exam Adapting the college experience for the AI era will be an ongoing process, and there is much work to do. But these examples demonstrate what’s both possible and necessary in higher education. Our job as educators is to teach students how to approach AI critically, help them see what AI can’t answer, and practice using these tools effectively. When these students graduate and are out in the world, employers will certainly expect that they have these skills already. Globally, jobs that require advanced AI skills are growing eight times faster than the job market overall, according to an analysis from the professional-services network PwC. At the same time, entry-level roles in AI-exposed fields are more than seven times likelier than other entry-level jobs to require the human skills once reserved for senior executives, including discernment, creativity, and leadership. Never before has the ability to harmonize critical thinking and technology mattered more for graduates. There will not—and should not—be a one-size-fits-all solution to the challenges of supplying this education. One professor may decide that students should write their essays in a blue book in class, while another may ask them to use an AI tool and audit every step of this work. Both choices will serve students when faculty are clear about their ambitions and standards. But outright bans or vague curbs afford no opportunities for students to better understand how to work ethically and effectively with AI. Seventy years ago, pioneering computer scientists launched the field of artificial intelligence on Dartmouth’s campus. That history gives our institution some responsibility for what comes next—not just to prepare students for a future defined by AI, but to give them the tools to help shape that future themselves. If colleges and universities sit out this revolution, we abdicate a sacred commitment that has guided American higher education for centuries: to prepare the next generation of leaders to navigate an ever-changing, always complicated, and frequently perilous world. In a world that promises to be defined by AI, universities that fail to produce graduates who can use it productively will consign themselves to irrelevance.

Crypto Money Still Matters for the Midterms
2026-09-04

Crypto Money Still Matters for the Midterms

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. The crypto industry has always moved in cycles: booms and busts; bull markets and harsh, unforgiving “winters.” We’re in something of a lull right now: Bitcoin, the original cryptocurrency, is worth about two-thirds of what it was last fall. Meanwhile, investors are turning their attention to flashier innovations in artificial intelligence. About half of Americans now use AI chatbots, whereas just about a fifth of the country has ever used or invested in crypto. But the crypto industry isn’t dead—as of today, the combined value of all digital currencies is still more than $2.7 trillion—and neither are its political ambitions. Crypto companies have so far put $206 million toward this year’s midterm elections, more than any other corporate sector, including the AI industry, according to a Public Citizen analysis of OpenSecrets and Federal Election Commission data. Fairshake, the industry’s foremost super PAC, has a giant war chest and a reputation for backing winners from both parties (Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer has reportedly warned of the risks of crossing it). Crypto is much further from the spotlight now than it was during its 2024 election-spending spree; back then, the industry was flush with cash, but its reputation was still recovering from the crash of two years prior and from the spectacle of Sam Bankman-Fried’s fraud trial. Even as Americans continue to distrust crypto, they’re now reserving their outrage for other corners of the tech industry, such as data centers and surveillance cameras. Although crypto is no longer as central to the public conversation, it’s still finding ways to flex its power. Fairshake first established itself as a major force during the 2024 election cycle, spending about $133 million in support of its chosen candidates. It started 2026 with a reported $193 million in the bank and has so far spent close to $70 million elevating politicians with an industry-friendly agenda. Fairshake’s record this year has been incredibly strong. Despite a few fairly significant losses, 50 of the 54 candidates it backed ended up winning their race. Not all of them won entirely because of Fairshake’s intervention: Some were incumbents who didn’t have a serious challenger. But an endorsement from Fairshake and its affiliate PACs tends to come with major financial support. The group spent more than $12 million to get a pro-crypto Republican into the general election for Alabama’s open Senate seat—it was “by far the biggest spender” in the primary, per The New York Times. And Protect Progress, Fairshake’s Democrat-focused PAC, spent more than $5 million to make another pro-crypto politician the party’s nominee for the House in Maryland’s Fifth District. Some candidates have incorporated these extreme interventions into their campaign strategy, accusing crypto-backed opponents of being in the industry’s pocket. Minnesota’s Peggy Flanagan, who won the state’s Democratic primary for the Senate, bashed her opponent for being pro-crypto and for being “funded by crypto and AIPAC and health-insurance companies.” In Florida’s Twenty-Fourth District, Fairshake spent more than $2 million trying to defeat Oliver Gilbert’s bid for the House, but Gilbert won his primary anyway. “Trump and his crypto con-artist billionaires just put a target on my back,” he said in one ad. Bloomberg reports that political candidates have purchased more ads mentioning the word crypto this year than they have in any of the past five years, and that the majority of these candidates are anti-crypto. Voters have never been all that passionate about crypto, and the Americans who do care about the tech are still disinclined to trust it. But the country’s attention has been elsewhere lately. Three-quarters of Americans say they’d oppose the construction of a data center in their community. Local governments across the country are now canceling contracts for Flock’s AI-enabled license-plate readers over rising fears of improper surveillance. In a bipartisan effort, 47 states, plus Washington, D.C., and U.S. territories, recently took Meta to trial for allegedly damaging “the mental and physical wellbeing of America’s youth” with its social-media platforms (the company just settled and will pay up to $17.1 billion). Although Fairshake’s spending spree has drawn attention to the role of crypto in politics, there’s been no equivalent public movement this year against the sector. When Politico asked Cynthia Lummis, arguably the country’s most pro-crypto senator, whether voters care about crypto, she replied, “No.” To get a sense of how much the industry’s public profile has shifted, it’s useful to think back on the past six years. Permit me a short history lesson. During the coronavirus pandemic, a speculative bubble massively boosted the industry’s profile. Then, in 2022, everything melted down. Bitcoin lost more than 60 percent of its value, and a series of high-profile corporate implosions created chaos throughout the sector. Executives from several major crypto companies were charged with fraud, but it was FTX’s Bankman-Fried who became, at least for a while, the industry’s criminal mascot. Last year, crypto ended up with a new mascot in the form of Donald Trump. Once in office, he threw his support behind the industry, implementing a deregulatory agenda and signing a major crypto bill into law. As cryptocurrencies soared, he also personally made $1.4 billion from his family’s businesses in the sector, leading to widespread allegations of corruption. As digital currencies have come back down to earth, they have started to feel more mundane. Crypto persists in partnerships with major financial institutions, in exchange-trading funds, and in the payment apps you may already have on your phone. None of this is particularly exciting—we’re unlikely to see an NFT at the Met Gala next year, as we did in 2021, or at the upcoming MTV Video Music Awards, as we did in 2022—yet it’s still a massive industry. Without that same level of visibility, crypto interests aren’t necessarily a public “target the way they were with the fallout of FTX, but on the other hand, they’ve had to struggle to stay relevant,” Mark Hays, an associate director at Americans for Financial Reform (a crypto-critical watchdog), told me; one of their strategies is to “continue to spend heavily to show that they still have clout.” The industry has plenty of battles left to fight, though—and plenty of cash to put toward winning them. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: You have, by now, probably come across the notion of maxxing—looks-maxxing, sleep-maxxing, protein-maxxing, potassium-maxxing, sun-maxxing, testosterone-maxxing. Etymologically speaking, maxxing seems to be a descendant of somewhat recent slang—for those of us who remember the late 20th century, “maxin’ and relaxin’ ” and “to the max” both come to mind—but in practice, maxxing is a reductionist worldview cloaked in expansionist language. The term is often invoked to describe a narrowly focused goal: If you’re maxxing, you’re likely trying to make yourself smaller, sharper, more chiseled. Maxxing requires sacrifice. It requires careful measurement. And in this way, maxxing is a consequence of our broader optimization-obsessed culture, a fixation fueled by every product, device, app, and service that trains people to collect and analyze data generated by their own behavior. Mark your calendars. The Permanent Questions (launching September 13) is a new podcast featuring the Atlantic staff writer David Brooks in conversation with the world’s deepest thinkers. Watch (or skip). Onslaught (in theaters now) is a victim of Hollywood’s obsession with turning everything into a franchise, David Sims writes. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. There’s No Such Thing as an AI ‘Lab’
2026-09-04

There’s No Such Thing as an AI ‘Lab’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
When did AI companies become AI labs? That term, and the image of studious, exacting white-coated scientists that it conjures, has become curiously entrenched as a descriptor for the businesses swiftly escorting the world into an existentially unsettling future. AI companies themselves have embraced the label: When Chris Olah, a co-founder of Anthropic, addressed the pope earlier this year, he repeatedly referred to his company, one of the most powerful AI enterprises in the world, as a lab. Industry press releases, technology commentators, and news publications have all picked up the word too. You’ll see the term on transit advertisements in San Francisco and in the pages of The New York Times (and, yes, The Atlantic). Here is my proposal: Everyone, please stop. This term has gone too far. Although AI companies do invest heavily in research and development, using a word typically associated with groups of truth-seeking scientists is misleading, odd, and too favorable to these companies’ own ends. As a software engineer working in tech, I’ve seen little evidence that AI companies’ research practices differ significantly from those of the rest of the tech industry. Google and Microsoft, for example, have robust research divisions, but we still call them companies. So why has the laboratory term become so deeply enmeshed with AI? Part of the reason is historical. Over the past century, research laboratories have played a crucial role in computer science. Studies performed at Bell Labs, one of America’s first industrial labs, resulted in the development of the transistor and the cellular network. In the 1970s, scientists at the MIT Laboratory for Computer Science pioneered the foundations of modern cryptography. As the Harvard historian Steven Shapin writes in his book The Scientific Life, countless innovations such as these that originated in corporate labs during the 20th century helped change the public’s perception of science. No longer simply knowledge for knowledge’s sake, science became viewed as a key engine of societal and economic progress. In their early years, OpenAI and Anthropic mainly performed research, similar to other industrial labs. Since then, however, they have commercialized rapidly: Each company is now valued near $1 trillion and generates billions in quarterly revenue. Holding on to the lab label seems to have the obvious PR benefit of allowing both companies to lean into their scientific legacy. Because of concerns about child safety, privacy, and job displacement, Americans largely hold a negative perception of the industry—and tech as a whole. That’s translated into popular opposition from politicians, data-center activists, and religious leaders who are concerned about the outsize influence of these companies: Spending on AI as a share of U.S. GDP now rivals defense spending. Meanwhile, scientists remain relatively popular. A 2026 Pew Research Center poll found that more than 75 percent of Americans trust scientists to act in the public interest, but the majority do not trust business leaders to do the same. These positive connotations associated with scientific research help the AI industry signal altruism while downplaying their profit-making ambitions. OpenAI and Anthropic’s leaders write at length about how AI’s risks and perils must be carefully studied for the sake of humanity. Research is portrayed as essential to this mission: OpenAI says it treats “safety as a science,” and Anthropic writes that “providing trustworthy research” will improve policy outcomes. The problem is, these companies act much more like private enterprises than bastions of science. Much of the research listed in the Publications section on both Anthropic’s and OpenAI’s websites are not peer-reviewed papers accepted by scholarly outlets. I examined 100 recent pieces of research from both companies’ Research pages; the overwhelming majority had no corresponding peer-reviewed paper accepted by a conference or journal. It’s unclear whether these were submitted to academic venues and, if so, whether they were rejected, are under review, or are forthcoming. (Neither OpenAI nor Anthropic responded to requests for comment.) Legal risks and long peer-review cycles are typically cited as reasons that fast-moving tech companies seldom share data or engage in peer review. And to its credit, the industry doesn’t always choose research topics that paint its products in a positive light. Anthropic has conducted studies on its models’ undesirable behaviors, such as disempowerment and sycophancy. Even so, Anthropic has a habit of landing on ambiguity about the fallout of its technology. In its education report, for instance, Anthropic, the entity best equipped to observe whether students are using Claude to cheat, concludes that the question of “How much are students using AI to cheat?” is “hard to answer.” The practice of assuming scientific authority while exercising potentially biased research practices has more in common with quasi-scientific advocacy organizations than it does with traditional laboratories. Take, for example, the George C. Marshall Institute, a think tank founded in 1984 whose leaders worked closely with the Reagan White House. The institute, whose publications defended oil interests and the tobacco industry, operated under the motto “Science for better public policy.” As the historians Naomi Oreskes and Erik Conway reported in the book Merchants of Doubt, the institute strategically refused to publish in journals subject to independent peer review. Instead, it disseminated reports and briefings to policy makers that merely reproduced the “trappings of scientific argumentation—graphs, charts, references, and the like.” If Anthropic, OpenAI, and other AI giants want to keep referring to themselves as labs, they should adhere to scientific best practices: open-sourcing code, preregistering studies, and, most important, subjecting research to independent peer review. Ultimately, AI companies such as Anthropic and OpenAI aren’t tweedy labs doing arcane research—they’re industry-defining corporations reshaping our workplaces, our schools, and our communities. Holding them accountable could start with calling them out for what they are—and what they aren’t.

Trump Has the Opportunity to Do the Funniest Thing
2026-09-05

Trump Has the Opportunity to Do the Funniest Thing

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Surge, the newsletter that covers the most important political nonsense of the week, delivered to your inbox every Saturday. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that has no time for a joke right here because we have important business to discuss. Starting next week, the Surge is going to look different: sleeker and fresher but still phenomenally trenchant. Alongside a refreshed design, the Surge will break down the news of the week into three categories: What you need to pay attention to now, what you need to keep an eye on, and which story is a bunch of nonsense you should ignore. Also, starting in October, the Surge will be available exclusively to Slate Plus subscribers. If you would like to subscribe to Slate Plus and the many riches it offers, use promo code SURGE25 at checkout for a 25 percent discount on a full-year subscription. We appreciate you, Surge Nation. Anyway, enough with the business talk. Let’s chat about something fun, like American politics, and its most important feature: money. MAGA Inc. is the president’s super PAC. As of July 31, it had $403 million on hand. The Trump associates who run it have kept telling and telling and telling House and Senate members that the balance will be spent on their races this fall, a cash bomb to help save GOP majorities in Congress. But it is now September of an election year, and the super PAC has thrown only a few bucks here and there. Where’s the cash? The Atlantic reported last week that Donald Trump was planning to spend soon, but with a twist: He had ordered “a series of television advertisements about his own record that will serve as a backdrop to the hundreds of individual House and Senate contests.” In other words, he called for ads celebrating himself. As you can guess, given Trump’s yawning unpopularity, this idea is going over poorly with the traditional GOP consultant class. Beyond that, will MAGA Inc. unload in individual races, mentioning—or attacking—individual candidates? Given that it is, again, September, Republicans are starting to doubt they’ll see much of this money. As the Washington Post reported, GOP leaders have begun to “conclude that it’s too late to fully deploy the cash in the midterms and that he intends to hold on to much of it for his own purposes.” This, dear reader. This is … it’s perfect, isn’t it? The single candidate most in need of MAGA Inc.’s largesse is Texas Senate candidate Ken Paxton. Given all of his scandals, baggage, and poor personal fundraising abilities, Paxton could use a heavy infusion of cash to defeat Democratic candidate James Talarico in a large, expensive state with umpteen media markets. But ever since Trump endorsed Paxton over Sen. John Cornyn (who had a much easier, and cheaper, path to victory) in the primary, Senate Republicans and big Texas donors have been of the mind that it’s Trump’s responsibility—not theirs—to fund his campaign. And so we have a bit of a standoff here. A Texas-size standoff. (Sorry, couldn’t resist. Last week of Surge 1.0!) The campaign to push MAGA Inc. to get involved there showed signs of urgency this week. Senate Majority Leader John Thune said on Fox News that “we’re going to need the president and his team” to get involved. Louisiana Sen. John Kennedy said he was “hoping that the president’s going to spend $100 or $200 million in Texas.” And the dean of Texas strategists, Karl Rove, warned that if Trump doesn’t get involved soon, “Talarico is going to run the message of ‘My opponent is the most corrupt politician in Texas history’ all the way to the victory lane.” All well and good. But have any of these complainers considered some of the nice golden trims Trump could use for his presidential library if he keeps this money? Let’s get priorities straight here. Here’s when we’ll know that artificial intelligence has achieved superintelligence: when we tell ChatGPT “Come up with a political event that the Surge could not possibly look forward to less” and it responds “The Republican midterm convention in Dallas.” (So far, the cowardly robot refuses to “invent one.”) Alas, the convention is happening next week for two days, and our dim view of the whole shebang appears to be widely shared within the GOP. Politico surveyed 70 GOP candidates and members and found that most of them weren’t going because they had convenient scheduling conflicts. As for the programming, the GOP intends to highlight its work on tax cuts and border security, as well as its support for things like stock trading bans for members of Congress. (There is no apparent programming for anti-corruption measures within the West Wing.) Now, there is a world where this could be productive for Republicans if they stuck to the script. But Trump, who was at first scheduled to speak only the second night, has now decided that he will speak both nights, ensuring that his showmanship will dominate all coverage. The good news for the GOP, at least, is that Trump’s speeches on both nights will be drowned out by the first two NFL games of the season. In the Massachusetts Senate primary on Tuesday, 80-year-old Sen. Ed Markey, who was first sent to Washington the year Jimmy Carter won the presidency, handily defeated a primary challenge from Rep. Seth Moulton. Our colleague Ian Prasad Philbrick wrote an excellent breakdown of the result this week. In a state like Massachusetts, where the Democratic campaign strategy is simple—run your ass to the left and never look back—Markey had the progressive bona fides to survive Democratic primary voters’ War on Old Incumbents. Moulton, meanwhile, had coded himself as unforgivably centrist, if not viciously right-wing, by saying, after the 2024 election, that he didn’t believe that trans women should play in women’s sports. This marks the second consecutive time Old Man Markey has fended off a former rising star in the party, having tanked Joe Kennedy III’s challenge in 2020. We would find it unusual if Markey ran again in 2032. If he does, though, good luck to whichever next-big-thing in Massachusetts politics chooses to end their career by primarying him. Two House Democrats—Washington Rep. Marie Gluesenkamp Perez and Maine Rep. Jared Golden—broke ranks with their party this week on a procedural vote teeing up votes on several bills, allowing the procedural vote to pass. The reason they did so was to ensure they got a vote on a bill that would help lobstermen. But their procedural votes also set up a vote on a messaging bill from Republicans that both condemned socialism “in all its forms” (lol) and called for the enactment of the SAVE America Act, Trump and Republicans’ hard-line elections bill. It’s a typical election-year poison-pill resolution that Republicans can use to run campaign ads saying Democrats voted to support socialism. Democratic members in competitive races did not want to vote on it but then had to, thanks to their friends from Washington and Maine. House Democrats were furious, and sass was thrown around freely. House Democratic Leader Hakeem Jeffries said he didn’t get a heads-up from the members, telling reporters, “You’ll have to talk to Katherine”—as in Rep. Katherine Clark, the whip. Rep. Nancy Pelosi, against whom Jeffries will always be measured, said that when she was in charge, “we didn’t even consider that anybody would do that.” Rep. Jim McGovern, the top Democrat on the Rules Committee, turned his back to Gluesenkamp Perez while she testified in the committee the following day. Members are so angry that the party is considering punishments for the members—Golden will be leaving Congress in a hot minute and got his lobster bill passed, so he couldn’t care less—and new caucus rules to ensure that such things don’t happen again. That might be a little heavy-handed when a simple talking-to, or a vote-whipping operation that’s more on top of things, could suffice. Last week, we wrote about Pete Hegseth’s firing of Gen. Randy George, the Army chief of staff, over a repeated series of clashes, including the question of whether a military helicopter fly-by at Kid Rock’s house was worthy of investigation. This week, Army Secretary Dan Driscoll submitted his resignation, a move that “had been seen as increasingly imminent after months of friction with Hegseth and others aligned with the Pentagon chief,” per the Post. What’s interesting about this one is that Driscoll has friends in places above Hegseth’s pay grade. He’s friends with Vice President J.D. Vance, with whom he attended Yale Law School. And when Driscoll personally brought his concerns about Hegseth’s leadership to Trump last week, Trump both encouraged Driscoll to stay on and reportedly “expressed concern about the deep cuts to the Army’s senior leadership.” Could this be the beginning of Hegseth losing favor with Trump? We’re going to say … no, Trump probably forgot about it in five minutes, when he resumed thinking about marble samples for the ballroom. Good news: These stupid midterms will be over soon, and then we can turn to the stupid presidential race for our yuks. On the GOP side, don’t expect it to simply be a race between Secretary of State Marco Rubio and Vance. Among others, Sen. Ted Cruz, who has waited three long cycles to give it another go after placing second to Trump in 2016, is making moves. As the Wall Street Journal reported from Iowa this week, Cruz has been “quietly asking people if they would be interested in working on his 2028 bid.” For some time now, too, he’s been pushing back on elements of MAGA, such as the growing antisemitism in the Tucker Carlson wing of the far right and overreaches by the Federal Communications Commission. In other words, he’s trying to carve a lane for himself as the representative of pre-Trump conservatism. Such a strategy depends on there being a backlash within the GOP to Trump-style populism, as well as general sloppiness as his presidency wanes. All we can say is, uh, good luck, we guess? The main reason we’re posting this is because Vance was asked about a Cruz 2028 run on Thursday and had, essentially, the same response as us: “And on Ted Cruz, I mean, look, man, he’s just clearly—Ted is a very authentic and likable guy, and I’m sure he’ll do great.” A deluge of cattiness awaits us in 2027. It’s past time to say it: This Supreme Court is helping President Donald J. Trump evade accountability. Erwin Chemerinsky, dean of the Berkeley School of Law, joins Dahlia Lithwick to break down the conservative majority’s procedural toolkit—standing, ripeness, the Purcell principle, and inscrutable rulings on the shadow docket—and how these doctrinal headfakes are being used to ensure Trump always wins in court, even when he loses. They dig into the White House ballroom case and Trump’s attempt to disrupt mail-in voting and the midterms, they explore what’s different from Trump’s first term, and they ask which of the justices are worried about the court’s legitimacy. Plus, we remember Gloria Steinem, who died at 92 this week. From co-founding Ms. magazine to her lifelong allyship with the civil rights movement, Steinem’s legacy of building coalitions, lifting up others, and never letting anyone steal her joy carries urgent lessons for a moment in which so much of what she fought for is being erased. Dahlia talks with legal scholar and Ms. Studios executive producer Michele Goodwin. Trump’s unleashing chaos on mail voting for the midterms. Can the courts stop him?
