Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.
2026-09-04

Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Maria Bartiromo, the conservative TV host known for her staunch support of Donald Trump, is out at Fox News. Generally speaking, we at the Slatest try to avoid getting wrapped up in palace intrigue, especially media palace intrigue. But this particular ouster offers an important window into how things really work at Fox—as well as into the broader conservative media ecosystem in which the network is the apex predator. Details are still trickling out, but here’s what we know. On Thursday, Fox announced that Bartiromo was “no longer with” the network, effective immediately. It didn’t give a reason, but reporters quickly put the pieces together. The drama seems to have started in July, when a Fox executive told the network’s on-air personalities to shy away from talking about a major Trump speech about election security. In that primetime address, the president floated conspiratorial insinuations about other countries interfering in the 2020 election, which he claims was stolen from him. Bartiromo, who has covered Trump’s election lies before, apparently forwarded the Fox executive’s guidance to the White House—“as if to say it wasn’t her fault that the claims weren’t getting airtime,” CNN reports—and Fox reportedly labeled Bartiromo’s disclosure a violation of her employment agreement. She’s contesting the emerging narrative—and maybe her apparent ouster, too. “The irresponsible reports that have been published stating that Maria Bartiromo was fired or is no longer an employee of Fox are absolutely and unequivocally false,” Bartiromo’s lawyer said today. “Make no mistake, we have the receipts and witnesses and they will come out, whether through the courthouse or otherwise.” Spicy! But assuming the reporting is right, is Bartiromo really the first Fox employee to get too close to the White House? No. During his first term, the president reportedly consulted the network’s on-air personalities for advice, sometimes even putting them on speakerphone in the Oval Office. One regular confidant was Sean Hannity, who even appeared briefly at a campaign rally with the president. Yet Hannity remains a Fox News host in good standing, and the network’s airwaves have become even more of a revolving door since Trump retook power. Several top officials this term—including Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Jeanine Pirro, the U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia—were previously Fox personalities. Nor does Bartiromo seem like the only Fox insider who’s passed along sensitive internal info. Roger Ailes, the network’s former CEO, reportedly tipped off Trump’s 2016 campaign about a question later posed by a Fox reporter, and Hannity even hid the fact that he’d apparently shared a lawyer with the president (Michael Cohen, who’s now back in Trump’s good graces). Hang on: Fox (reportedly) fired Bartiromo! So doesn’t that prove the network isn’t just an arm of the White House? Trump’s 2020 election lies are among his most consistently expressed beliefs. Declining to give them airtime—and cutting ties with a host who has promoted them—certainly looks like an act of courage. But it has to be considered in the context of a network that constantly promotes many of Trump’s other lies. And, as noted, network personnel have been caught colluding with him. So what’s different here? There may be a baser motive at play: money. Bartiromo’s zealous coverage of the president’s claims had become a legal headache, helping cost Fox hundreds of millions of dollars in one defamation suit. Oh yeah. Remind me what happened in that case? In 2021, Dominion Voting Systems sued Fox for airing unsubstantiated allegations—including on Bartiromo’s show—that the company’s ballot-tabulation machines were rigged to steal the election. Just as the case was about to go to trial, Fox settled for $787 million. Fox is also facing a similar suit from another voting machine company, Smartmatic, in which Bartiromo is a defendant. According to NPR, Fox’s lawyers went to court for a hearing in that case on Thursday—the same day the network announced Bartiromo’s departure. (Bartiromo’s wouldn’t be the first Trump-friendly head to roll over the president’s election lies. The network ousted longtime host Lou Dobbs after Smartmatic sued and parted ways with Tucker Carlson, who also aired Trump’s false claims, days after the Dominion settlement.) Ouch. But what does this episode tell us about conservative media in general? It leads to some pretty cynical conclusions about the Murdochs, the billionaire family that owns Fox News, the New York Post, and other organs of the right-wing information ecosystem. Rupert Murdoch, who officially handed off his media empire to his son Lachlan last year, arguably helped create the political substrate that germinated Trump, who regularly appeared on Fox to criticize Barack Obama years before running for president. The Trump era has also been good for the Murdochs’ properties. Fox remains the country’s most-watched cable news network, and its other hosts regularly carry water for Trump’s agenda. So rather than taking a stand for the truth or against the president, Fox seems to have ditched Bartiromo to save face—and maybe cash. Trump isn’t pleased; “her fans, of which there are many, will not be happy,” he wrote in a valedictory Truth Social post. Yet Bartiromo’s ouster seems unlikely to break the symbiotic relationship that continues to benefit both the president and his favorite network. In response to questions about why she was let go, Fox called it “a business decision.” Indeed. It’s the holiday weekend, so you, like Bartiromo, likely have some extra free time on your hands. My colleagues have some great suggestions for how to spend it. Go to the movies: If you’re planning a trip to the theater this weekend, allow us to offer some suggestions. If you want to watch a comedy, check out Coyote vs. Acme. If you’re looking for action and adventure, Sam calls The End of Oak Street a “nasty little throwback to Jurassic Park.” For horror fans, It Ends will shake you to your core. If you’re in the mood for a coming-of-age drama, the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony is “is a perfect little movie,” according to Dana Stevens. We would recommend skipping The Dog Stars and The Whisper Man, which were both disappointing adaptations of great books. Binge a new TV show: If you’d rather stay in and stream something, we have a couple of recommendations. Sterling Point is a summer romance series that “will stand the test of time,” according to Rebecca Onion. Rebecca also raves about the crime thriller Furious, which features one of the best performances on TV right now. Lanterns, writes Sam Adams, “is a superhero show for people who think they don’t like superhero shows.” Sam was also pleasantly surprised by the newest season of Ted Lasso, which bounced back after its previous dreadful season. Finally, if you are craving some juicy reality TV, Scaachi Koul dubbed TLC’s Double Lives of Suburban Wives, which follows a group of Christian moms doing sex work, an “absolute banger.” Celebrate Labor Day with a new tradition: Sorry to Bother You, Boots Riley’s 2018 film starring LaKeith Stanfield, is a rollicking sci-fi black comedy about a dystopian workplace. It is also, argues Terri Gerstein, the perfect movie to rewatch this weekend. She argues that what It’s a Wonderful Life is to Christmas and Groundhog Day is to Groundhog Day, Sorry to Bother You should be to Labor Day. Sample some new snacks: Summer barbecues are rife with potato-chip mediocrity. This weekend, leave the predictable bag of chips behind and win the cookout with thrilling bites from Thailand, Canada, and even … Pennsylvania. Danny Palumbo recommends the best snacks to enliven your cookout. Or, if you prefer softer spuds, Danny also has a genius tip for leveling up your potato salad. As the last thing standing between you and a long weekend, let me get out of your way. We’re taking Labor Day off, so I’ll be back in your inbox on Tuesday. ’Til then! The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?
2026-09-04

The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The case of Lindsay Clancy has officially ended in a mistrial. The high-profile murder case has consumed national media—for better and for worse—for weeks now, as a jury considered whether the Massachusetts mother intentionally took the lives of her three children or if her crime (and punishment) could be mitigated by reason of insanity. After the jury spent nearly 40 hours deliberating, the judge declared a mistrial on Friday afternoon. That means this case is far from over, with plenty of options, and questions, remaining about Clancy’s fate—and if and how the commonwealth of Massachusetts will try her again. For some broader context: Clancy’s case was exceptionally rare, as she pleaded guilty to strangling her three children back in 2023 but argued that she should not be held criminally liable because she was suffering from postpartum psychosis at the time. The prosecution argued that she was able to parse right from wrong and preplanned to commit the atrocities at the Clancy family home. The jury, made up of nine women and three men, spent six days deliberating the case and twice sent notes to the judge indicating they could not reach a unanimous verdict. Kevin Reddington, Clancy’s defense attorney, suggested that a sole juror had admitted to feeling there was reasonable doubt concerning her guilt but refused to join the 11 jurors who were ready to find Clancy not guilty, per her insanity defense. On Friday, as Judge William Sullivan was announcing a mistrial, Reddington asked to appeal his decision with the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court. Sullivan obliged, allowing Reddington one hour to file his appeal, but it ultimately failed, with the state Supreme Court quickly holding a hearing and denying the application for an emergency stay. To unpack the other complicated details of this case’s non-ending, I spoke with Effie Blassberger, a trial attorney at Clayman Rosenberg Kirshner & Linder who focuses on #MeToo–related litigation alongside white-collar criminal defense. Here is our conversation, which has been edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: The jury’s landing point in this case was described by Clancy’s attorney as an “intractable position.” What does that mean, specifically within the context of reasonable doubt? Effie Blassberger: It’s basically saying that one juror has a fixed view and it’s not moving. The judge sent the jury back to deliberate several times, and the idea behind juror deliberations is you’re supposed to discuss the evidence with one another with candor, to openly listen. But it sounds like there was one juror whose position is intractable, that they have this fixed view that isn’t changing, no matter how much more deliberations continue. When it comes to reasonable doubt, if a juror actually believes there’s reasonable doubt, that’s supposed to work in Clancy’s favor. That’s the whole point of the standard. Reasonable doubt isn’t something you just note and move past. It’s supposed to end the jury’s deliberations in the defendant’s favor. Reddington suggested that the holdout juror did acknowledge doubt but still wanted to convict anyway. That’s his whole argument, that this one juror wasn’t following the law as instructed and he wanted to have him removed. But that being said, you have to keep in mind that we are getting this information secondhand—it’s a juror note that’s being interpreted and characterized by Reddington. This is not a note from the holdout juror. It’s coming from the point of view of jurors who are frustrated with this lone juror who is not agreeing with them and their characterization of this juror. How unusual is it for there to be one holdout juror that ends up overtaking a jury’s decisionmaking like this? I think it’s less unusual than people think. Hung juries do happen across criminal trials; obviously, they are more rare and infrequent. However, when a jury hangs, it’s usually in this exact sort of situation, where it’s not an even split—it’s one person who won’t move. It is also common and happens throughout every jury trial that jurors send notes. That’s how they communicate with the judge and the lawyers. Some of those notes might ask that they see certain evidence again, they might want to see certain transcripts, they might need the judge to instruct them on the law. Sometimes, through questions that jurors are asking, both the judge and the lawyers might gain some insights into what the jurors are thinking in a particular case, and it certainly seems that that’s what happened here. My understanding is that Reddington has, through these notes, come to his own assessment that except for this holdout juror, there’s an 11-to-1 split leaning toward not guilty by reason of lack of criminal responsibility. We know that Reddington asked the judge to have this one juror thrown out, but the judge said no. What considerations do judges make in these types of situations? It’s certainly rare that somebody’s trying to remove one juror at this stage of the case, and it’s important to grasp why he’s trying to do that. The only way to do it is by saying: Look, this juror is not following his oath. He’s not able to follow the law. That’s my understanding that was gleaned from one of the juror notes. However, simply because the juror is holding out and not agreeing with other jurors, that’s not a basis to remove them. It’s a very delicate balance here because this whole system works by respecting the integrity of the deliberations and the process. If this one juror just had strong convictions that happen to be different from everyone else’s, the law allows that, and it’s not for a judge or anyone else to intervene after the jury has been instructed to try to come to a consensus. The judge’s only role here is to make sure the jurors are following the law, and unless he had reason to believe that this juror was not following and applying the law, he was not going to intervene. If it was so clear-cut that the juror was actually saying, “I have reasonable doubt, but I want to convict,” the judge’s decision about whether there was good cause to remove the juror would have been much more straightforward. But that’s clearly not what happened here. I don’t think it was as simple as Reddington, perhaps, is characterizing the note, or as the media is characterizing the note. Remember, Reddington seemed to think that but for this lone juror, the verdict would have been in his client’s favor. He went down the only avenue that was available to him after he made his motion to appeal to the judge, and that motion was denied. My understanding is that it had a very low chance of success, but as a defense attorney you have to do everything you can for your client and to exhaust every possible avenue, and he did that. Reddington’s appeal failed, and Sullivan officially declared a mistrial. What might the prosecution do next? There are three options. They can retry the case with a brand-new jury. Or they could negotiate some sort of plea deal with Clancy. My understanding is that there might be limitations on what pleas are available based on the counts that are in this case. The last option would be to not pursue this case further. My understanding is that the district attorney’s office hasn’t said anything publicly yet about how they intend to pursue. My sense is, given how much public attention this case has gotten, they are going to retry her. It’s hard to envision that the prosecutors learned anything different about this trial. Presumably these were the choices that they had before, they could have tried her or offered a plea deal, and I don’t think the trial produced any new information that the prosecution learned that changed their view of this case. They believe that this was intentional and premeditated, and I think they’re going to stand by those beliefs. I don’t think the public attention is going to sway the prosecution, but finding a new jury who is impartial is going to be really challenging, because this case has received so much media attention. I think in the future, attorneys would try to screen this out during jury selection. You can ask questions to try to screen this out, and it’s worth pointing out that this is not the first time—there are cases that have received so much media attention. Those cases do get tried, but it’s certainly challenging. The DA presumably makes the final decision to retry Clancy. He’ll likely consult with others in his office, but ultimately it’s his decision to make. And he would try the same exact charges but have a new jury. She has been denied bail, so she’s been held in custody since 2023 at the Tewksbury State Hospital. Now that a mistrial has been declared, that does not equate to an acquittal. Nothing changes, at least immediately, in terms of where she stands; she’ll continue staying at Tewksbury. Her lawyer might try to have her bail reconsidered, but the fact that there’s been a mistrial doesn’t really change Clancy’s circumstances. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot
2026-09-04

Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. President Donald Trump’s executive order directing the post office to suppress mail voting has often been called “unprecedented.” That is not quite right. An earlier president—in fact, one of Trump’s role models—also empowered the Postal Service to squelch political opposition. It was one of the most disreputable episodes in U.S. history, and Trump has attempted to revive it with a modern twist. In the summer of 1835, the American Anti-Slavery Society began mailing a pamphlet titled Human Rights, along with other literature, to thousands of civic and religious leaders in Southern states, hoping to convince readers of the immorality of slavery. Things did not go as planned. That July, a shipment that included Human Rights, the Emancipator, and a children’s magazine called the Slave’s Friend arrived in Charleston, South Carolina. The local postmaster, a Jacksonian Democrat named Alfred Huger, was unnerved by what he considered inflammatory content and sequestered the abolitionist material, rather than comply with his legal obligation to deliver the mail. That evening, a mob of so-called lynch men broke into the warehouse and removed the mailbags, which they soon consigned to a great bonfire that attracted thousands of cheering spectators. Huger wrote for instructions to Postmaster General Amos Kendall, as well as New York Postmaster Samuel Gouverneur, from whose office the offending literature had been shipped. Gouverneur agreed to refuse acceptance of abolitionist shipments pending a ruling from Kendall. Kendall had been a key backer of Democrat Andrew Jackson in the 1828 election, having defected from Henry Clay’s Whigs. His reward was appointment to Jackson’s “Kitchen Cabinet,” in which he was widely considered one of the president’s most loyal advisers. After his reelection in 1832, Jackson nominated Kendall to be postmaster general. The appointment was controversial, as Jackson’s opponents feared the consequences of putting such a partisan figure in charge of the nation’s communications. Nonetheless, Kendall was confirmed. He did not let Jackson down. Kendall’s reply to the Charleston postmaster was careful but unmistakable. Acknowledging that he lacked statutory authority to ban material from the mail, he nonetheless ruled against delivery of the abolitionist periodicals. “By no act, or direction of mine, official or private, could I be induced to aid, knowingly, in giving circulation to papers of this description, directly or indirectly,” he explained. Jackson, a slaveholder and the brutal enforcer of Indian removal, enthusiastically agreed that the “wicked plan of exciting the negroes to insurrection and to massacre” had to be stopped. He instructed Southern postmasters to record and publicize the names of even individual subscribers to abolitionist newspapers to encourage intimidation by their neighbors and perhaps even compel them to leave the South. In his message to Congress that year, Jackson recommended legislation to prohibit, “under severe penalties, the circulation in the Southern States, through the mail, of incendiary publications intended to instigate the slaves to insurrection,” which of course meant any abolitionist pamphlets or newspapers. Jackson’s proposal died in the Senate, but that did not end postal censorship of abolitionist periodicals. Many Southern states passed their own laws and resolutions mandating rejection of abolitionist material, on the spurious claim that it tended “to excite the slaves of the southern states to insurrection and revolt.” Southern postmasters thus continued to withhold antislavery periodicals, with the tacit approval of Jackson protégé Kendall. The local suppression of abolitionist mail persisted throughout the antebellum era during the administrations of Jackson’s eight successors, none of whom enforced the federal statute prohibiting detention of the mail. In 1857, for example, Attorney General Caleb Cushing, appointed by President James Buchanan, opined that Mississippi law superseded the federal postal statute because of the state’s right, under the Constitution, to protect itself “against ‘domestic violence’ and to make provisions to ‘suppress insurrection.’ ” Consequently, the postmaster in Yazoo City, Mississippi, could not be required to deliver printed matter that tended “to produce disaffection, disorder and rebellion among the colored population of the state.” Both Jackson’s and Trump’s efforts to manipulate the mail have rested on fabricated premises. In Jackson’s case, it was the racist myth that enslaved people were content with their condition and would remain passive unless instigated by Northern abolitionists. In Trump’s case, it is the myth that mail ballots—the same method Trump uses to vote—are a form of election rigging. Or, as he put it with characteristic mendacity and capitalization, “ELECTIONS CAN NEVER BE HONEST WITH MAIL IN BALLOTS.” The slavocrats’ abuse of the Postal Service ended only with the Civil War. The Supreme Court has the power to terminate Trump’s voter-suppression scheme much sooner. We shall see. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. BepiColombo sheds its ride and starts the final glide to Mercury
2026-09-05

BepiColombo sheds its ride and starts the final glide to Mercury

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches After nearly eight years, ESA and JAXA's BepiColombo spacecraft is finally nearing Mercury, as engineers begin the arrival sequence by jettisoning the power module that got it this far. The spacecraft stack separated from the Mercury Transfer Module (MTM) on September 3 at around 1400 CEST. After a tense few minutes, controllers confirmed that a preliminary Doppler signal had been detected, indicating that the MTM had separated successfully; by 1552 CEST, it was confirmed that signals had been picked up, indicating that all had gone to plan. The solar panels of the Mercury Planetary Orbiter (MPO) were charging the spacecraft's batteries. The next stage involves dropping into orbit around Mercury on November 21. Then the Mercury Magnetospheric Orbiter (MMO) – aka Mio – will be released between December 9 and 10, and a week later MPO will start moving to its final orbit over 480 x 1500 km, with science commencing in April 2027. The arrival is a year later than planned. BepiColombo was supposed to have been orbiting Mercury in December 2025, but problems with the spacecraft's Solar Electric Power (SEP) system meant the trajectory had to be rethought, which extended the spacecraft's cruise. BepiColombo is no stranger to Mercury and has performed six flybys of the planet since 2021. It has also conducted flybys of Venus and Earth following its 2018 launch. The spacecraft is an impressive example of international cooperation. The MPO and MTM are ESA contributions, while the MMO comes from JAXA. ESA also provided the sunshield to protect the MMO during transit. This sunshield will be ejected after the MMO is released. The MPO is the big brother of the BepiColombo partnership, with a main body measuring 2.4 x 2.2 x 1.7 m and a mass of 1,230 kg (including an 85 kg science payload). JAXA's MMO is 1.8 m in diameter and 1.1 m high, and weighs 255 kg (45 kg of which is the science payload). While MPO is 3-axis stabilized and nadir-pointing, MMO will be spin-stabilized at 15 rpm, with its spin axis 90 degrees to the Sun. The goal of the mission is a study of Mercury, "from the structure and dynamics of its magnetosphere and how it interacts with the solar wind, to the properties of its large iron core and the origin of its magnetic field." The initial mission at Mercury is planned for one Earth year (approximately four Mercury years), although it could be extended by another Earth year. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. And more! Anthropic lays groundwork for bots that shop for you
2026-09-04

Anthropic lays groundwork for bots that shop for you

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases Anthropic has blueprints for building e-commerce bots that shop on behalf of clients. That's not something many consumers want at the moment, but the AI biz this week published a set of templates for Claude-based shopping agents and merchant agents in the hope of encouraging more e-commerce businesses to implement automated purchasing. Its blueprint "contains the harnesses, patterns, and guardrails an engineering team needs to get a commerce agent running in days, with reference implementations of a shopping agent and a merchant agent for retail, travel, telecom, and ticketing platforms," the company said. Large retailers and ecommerce platforms, Anthropic observes, are using Claude to build AI agents to simplify online shopping by allowing customers to pose natural language queries to find, compare, and buy items. The buying bit may be wishful thinking: A recent Gartner survey found that while consumers use AI to find products and conduct research, they're not ready to delegate purchasing authority to AI agents. The survey found just 11 percent of consumers are willing to let AI make purchase decisions. A recent Accenture survey [PDF] is a bit more optimistic, noting, "Three in four (74 percent) consumers would let an AI agent handle routine tasks; one in three (32 percent) are ready to hand over purchase decisions; and 9 percent are open to fully autonomous shopping, before the technology is fully operational." Either way, at least AI-assisted product finding and comparison appear to be plausible activities. Anthropic's GitHub repo contains functional shopping and merchant agents that can be built with the Messages API, Agent SDK, or Claude Managed Agents. The biz's shopping agent blueprint comes with ways to connect to product catalogs, online shopping carts, checkout systems, preference databases, and purchase history databases. "A customer can say 'I need a tent, sleeping bag, and stove for a weekend trip with two kids,' and the agent can take it from there," Anthropic suggests, noting that its code supports guardrails "designed to constrain prices and products to actual catalog data, and avoids manipulative upsell patterns." Why might prices need constraint? Well, beyond the possibility of hallucinated pricing, there's considerable concern about how AI agents have the potential to compound the problems with dynamic pricing – where customers are charged different amounts for the same goods. "The rise of agentic AI, where algorithms monitor online behavior to customize costs, will likely exacerbate dynamic pricing," said the Brookings Institution earlier this year. "These agents can assess individual behavior and tailor prices based on a consumer’s shopping and purchasing history." Concerns about AI surveillance pricing were discussed last month at a Senate Judiciary subcommittee hearing. In prepared remarks, Lindsay Owens, president and CEO of Groundwork Collaborative, a Washington, DC-based advocacy group, noted that already half of those using the Walmart app use "Sparky," the company's AI assistant. Those shoppers spend about 35 percent more than those who don't, Owens said. "These agents have access to swaths of data on customers – what shoppers tell them, what they’ve purchased in the past, and in some cases, what they’ve hovered over with their mouse or even what other websites they’ve visited," she said. "All of that can be used to infer exactly how much they’re willing to pay for a product and set the price tag accordingly." Even if shoppers can be assured of getting a fair deal using AI agents, there's another concern. Monica Eaton, founder and CEO of Chargebacks911, told The Register in an emailed statement that agentic commerce looks likely to create fraud problems for merchants. There isn't yet a framework for merchants to deal with AI purchases that consumers claim they didn't authorize. "Merchants cannot become the insurer of every misunderstanding between a consumer and their AI," she said. Anthropic's templates may prove helpful for AI agent design, but the real challenge isn't technical. It's about trust – consumers have to trust that AI will act in their best interest and merchants have to trust that AI will be accountable. That may take time. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. AMD's Threadripper Halo is a local-AI workstation for researchers with deep pockets
2026-09-04

AMD's Threadripper Halo is a local-AI workstation for researchers with deep pockets

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Machine learning researchers have a new and expensive option for doing local AI. AMD just raided its spare parts bin and cobbled together a DGX Station rival boasting up to 576 GB of HBM3e memory that it’s calling the Threadripper Halo. Announced at IFA 2026, the system, which is set to launch next year, marks the first time that AMD’s Instinct accelerators have been offered in a workstation form factor. But much like the AI Halo we reviewed earlier this year, the Threadripper Halo isn’t packing anything we haven’t seen before. The liquid-cooled system is powered by a 96-core Threadripper PRO 9995WX, which you may recall made its debut in 2025. The CPU is backed by up to 2 TB of DDR5 memory and up to 2.6 TB of combined system memory. As you probably already guessed, AMD is positioning the box as a local AI workstation for researchers. From what we gather, the system can be had with up to four of the company’s PCIe-based Instinct GPUs — though the systems displayed at IFA only showed two installed. Launched back in May, the MI350P is essentially half of an MI350X that’s been crammed into a PCIe form factor. Each accelerator packs 144 GB of speedy HBM3e good for 4 TB/s of memory bandwidth, and up to 4.6 petaFLOPS of FP4 compute. But we’ll note that it's in its 600 W configuration. When deployed in the Threadripper Halo, we expect AMD will be running the cards in the more sedate 450 W config. Combined, the Threadripper Halo promises up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth, enabling the system to run models exceeding a trillion parameters in size (at four-bit precision) entirely in GPU memory. By offloading some of the model to system memory, the system should be able to run the largest open weights models, like Moonshot.AI’s 2.8 trillion-parameter Kimi K3. The ability to run large frontier-class models on your desk won’t come cheap and may not be available in every market, at least not without an electrical service upgrade. AMD hasn’t shared pricing, but as configured, we expect it to sell for somewhere between $100,000 and $150,000. A quad MI350P system is going to push the limits of a standard North American power outlet unless AMD either underclocks the cards from 600 W to 300 W or it mandates a 20 amp circuit. This might explain why the system AMD showed off at IFA only had two. At this price and power level, the system is well positioned to compete with Nvidia’s own high-end AI workstation, the DGX Station. Announced at GTC in 2025, the DGX Station features a 252 GB B300 GPU and a 72-core Grace CPU along with 496 GB of LPDDR5x memory, and it retails for around $100,000 if you can find one in stock. The House of Zen boasts that the system offers up to 3.4x the total system memory and more than twice the memory bandwidth of the DGX Station, which should give an edge in LLM inference, assuming tensor parallel operations don’t bottleneck on the CPU’s PCIe bus. AMD says the Threadripper Halo will be available starting next year. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. ASCII smuggling isn't just an AI security risk
2026-09-04

ASCII smuggling isn't just an AI security risk

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters Fraudsters have found a new use for ASCII smuggling, typically used to hide malicious prompts intended for AI models, in an old-school attack method: email phishing. Microsoft uncovered a massive phishing campaign using invisible Unicode tag characters that peaked at more than 2.37 million messages in late February, remained elevated during weekdays over the next three months, and gradually declined by mid-June. “As AI-era attack methods become better understood, threat actors may adapt them for use in more traditional threats such as phishing and spam,” Redmond’s researchers Noam Kochavi and Sarah Wolstencroft said in a Thursday blog. “This case illustrates how techniques that emerge in AI security research can quickly cross over into established attack ecosystems, reinforcing the need for defenders to view emerging threats through a cross-domain lens.” ASCII smuggling involves using invisible or non-rendering Unicode characters to hide content inside text that appears normal to humans, and this makes it a popular technique for indirect prompt injection attacks. In these, an attacker hides instructions for an AI assistant in invisible Unicode characters, and embeds those malicious prompts inside a webpage or document. A human can’t see them, but a model can, and it decodes them as text - and may then follow the attacker’s instructions to leak data or take unauthorized actions. Instead of using ASCII smuggling for prompt injection, however, Microsoft’s security team spotted someone using invisible characters – inserting Unicode tag spaces between letters – to split financial-lure words in phishing emails in an apparent attempt to evade keyword matching and other content filters. So, for example, instead of writing “funding” in the email, the attackers wrote “fun⟨U+E0020⟩ding.” “When we looked at a sampling of the flagged messages, the surprise was there were no smuggled instructions to an AI assistant,” Kochavi and Wolstencroft wrote. “Instead, the invisible tag characters were inserted inside common financial keywords, splitting them apart so that a literal signature or keyword match would fail.” Redmond first detected the ASCII-smuggling signature in early February, flagging about 21,000 messages on February 8 before the number skyrocketed to more than 1.3 million the next day. Most of these emails came from about 150 finance-themed sender domains, and they continued for the next three months, dropping sharply after May 15 but continuing with occasional smaller spikes through at least mid-June. The security researchers pointed out two notable characteristics, including sending massive numbers of emails on weekdays and then going silent over the weekend. The campaign also had a long, gradual decline. “After an intense first phase, with weekday volumes of 1 to 2.37 million messages, peaking on February 26, the numbers stepped down slowly to roughly 80 percent less per weekday by late March.” It then dropped significantly after May 15, continuing with lower activity through mid-June. According to Redmond, the most important thing defenders can do to protect against Unicode tag blocks in phishing emails is to verify that normalization and tokenization pipelines handle tag characters consistently. “Any content that will be evaluated by keyword, signature, or regex logic should first have invisible and non-rendering Unicode code points stripped or folded, so that splicing them into a word no longer defeats the match,” the threat hunters wrote. This same control can also help reduce the threat of ASCII-smuggling against AI assistants that ingest email content. Microsoft also suggests scanning for behavioral indicators. “The observed activity had a distinctive shape: bulk volume from churning, finance-themed disposable domains, on a strict weekday-on / weekend-off schedule,” Kochavi and Wolstencroft warned. “A sudden spike of tag-block characters concentrated on finance-themed senders, switching on and off weekly, is a high-confidence campaign indicator.” ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Rogue OpenAI agents used dead German web site to communicate in May, months before Hugging Face incident
2026-09-04

Rogue OpenAI agents used dead German web site to communicate in May, months before Hugging Face incident

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Two cases of agents escaping to solve unsolvable problems paints an uncomfortable question: Is the entire internet in OpenAI's experimental agentic firing line? OpenAI’s agents were going rogue as early as May, according to a new report, making the Hugging Face incident far from the first where bots committed a breach. A report published Friday by a group of researchers claims to have found – with all of the agent posts presented as evidence – a self-identified “swarm” of OpenAI agents taking over a functionally dead German software developer wiki. Over the course of a month, from May to June, the agents made around 18,000 posts to the wiki, apparently acting against OpenAI developer intentions. According to the researchers, the wiki was hijacked like OpenAI’s own Artifactory package manager involved in the Hugging Face incident, to serve as a message board for agents. In this case, they appear to have been tasked with performing a timed web lookup task, for which the agents were apparently granted read access to the web, but not write access. One of the first things they apparently did was figure out how to subvert that restriction in order to post to the hijacked wiki. “The agents used this wiki to communicate information with each other, primarily to help them succeed at their task,” the researchers explained from a review of the thousands of posts made to the wiki, all of which are available for review in the report. “They asked for answers, pooled results, and shared techniques for bypassing their restrictions.” The agents also tried to pool their knowledge of the task they were assigned to predict what sort of questions they’d be asked next, and even set up heartbeat tasks to predict whether completing the assigned tasks would lead to their termination. They also discussed using Tor and other anonymizing services to hide their traffic, and began to react when a human moderator of the site spotted their traffic and started to delete posts. The researchers discovered all of this while searching for additional cases of rogue AI agent activity in the wake of the Hugging Face attack. In an email to The Register, OpenAI told us that it has acted with transparency and good faith around its agentic AI incidents, voluntarily disclosing such matters and maintaining a commitment to providing an accurate account of what its systems do. As for why it didn’t mention the German incident when it disclosed the Hugging Face one, that’s because the two were entirely unrelated, a spokesperson explained – as if that’s reassuring. OpenAI added that it should have been obvious from the Hugging Face report that this has happened multiple times, however, pointing us to a passage in its blog post on that incident. “While investigating this incident, we discovered rare cases in which agents without multi-agent tools found ways to collaborate via side channels during training,” the Hugging Face post-mortem explains. “We believe that this behavior started due to generalization from multi-agent training.” In other words, if we didn’t read between the lines, that’s on us. OpenAI declined to provide additional details, however. As was the case in the Hugging Face attack, one of the tasks assigned to the agents was impossible given the restrictions placed on it. With Hugging Face, it involved a cybersecurity task that was impossible to answer from within the agents’ sandboxes,. In this case, the researchers said the task was to provide a statistic accessible from a Power BI dashboard. Retrieving said statistics required the ability to make a GET request, but the agents were restricted to POST requests. In both cases, the OpenAI agents acted against their programming to escape their restrictions, ostensibly in an attempt to solve a problem put to them that couldn’t be done without acting against their operators. The agents in this case were able to collaborate and find a way around the restriction by exploiting a condition in their sandboxes that excepted Azure Blob Storage hostnames from a security proxy. When the agents realized this, they started directing traffic through those hostnames to avoid GET restrictions. It was through GET requests that the agents gained write permissions on the internet, kicking this whole thing off. The similar nature of both incidents, in which bots escaped their sandboxes and subverted restrictions when given an impossible-to-complete task, begs a very important question: Are OpenAI’s engineers so incompetent that they’d screw up fundamental instructions twice, or is the company intentionally hamstringing their agents to see what they’re capable of, with the entirety of the internet placed downrange? For that matter, how many more times do we need to read between the lines of OpenAI's corpo-speak to infer this has happened more than the two times we know about so far? OpenAI, predictably, didn’t respond to that line of questioning. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. My Word. Microsoft's prepping Similarity Checker for retirement
2026-09-04

My Word. Microsoft's prepping Similarity Checker for retirement

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Built-in plagiarism police to hand in badge next month Microsoft is retiring Word's Similarity Checker on October 3 and stripping the feature from Microsoft Editor. Other writing and editing tools, including Microsoft 365 Copilot, won't be affected. But if an organization uses Similarity Checker as part of its workflow, it's time to consider alternatives. Similarity Checker has been around for a few years. Launched in 2020, the Bing Search-powered service compared text from the user to online sources and came up with a percentage score – how much of the text was similar to those online sources. The user could then add citations if appropriate, or try again for originality. Microsoft had previously said: "This can aid writers in focusing less on the mechanics of writing, and more on the content." Word's citation capabilities will not be affected by the retirement, nor will its spelling and grammar checking tools. Although Microsoft's AI assistant is unaffected, Microsoft warned, "Copilot is not intended to serve as a formal similarity, plagiarism, or originality validation tool." Plagiarism spotting and similarity checking have become all the more important in recent years. Turnitin, for example, added features to identify AI-generated text, and there is evidence that plagiarism-detecting tools have had an impact (although there are plenty of other nefarious uses for AI.) The writing for the Similarity Checker in Word has been on the wall for a while now. Although Microsoft has not shared usage statistics, it did hint that users were giving it a swerve: "This change allows us to focus on experiences and capabilities that are more broadly used across Microsoft Word." The functionality is certainly at odds with the Copilot-All-The-Things fever that has gripped Microsoft in recent years, despite Microsoft's insistence that Copilot is not a tool for formal similarity checking. That said, there were a few alarmed users when the tool stopped working for a few days in 2023. After October 3, 2026, the Similarity Checker will stop working for good and cease to be accessible through the Editor experience in Word. ® Eight year after launch, Mercury probe and its Mercury Transfer Module consciously uncouple as orbit approaches Now comes the harder sell: Convincing customers and merchants to trust AI with purchases AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk Hacking education to thrive in the AI world is the first and best step in keeping control Phishers find a new use for invisible Unicode tag characters AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. The Verge
The weird and wonderful headphones of CanJam 2026
2026-09-05

The weird and wonderful headphones of CanJam 2026

The Verge | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
﻿Mainstream headphones are good but boring now. I went to an audiophile show to be reminded why I love headphones. ﻿Mainstream headphones are good but boring now. I went to an audiophile show to be reminded why I love headphones. I’ve been reviewing headphones for a long time, and I’ve listened to everything from the barely serviceable to multi-thousand-dollar open-back headphones. But recently I’ve been uninspired by the state of mainstream options. Most are perfectly good — great, even — but they lack a distinct character. Something to remind me why I fell in love with headphones to begin with, landing me in this crazy career. I needed some revitalization. I needed to see something cool. I needed to be around other people with an emotional connection to headphones. I needed to go to CanJam. CanJam is a small audio show produced by Head-Fi.org, the largest audio-focused online forum, with multiple events a year at different places around the world, including London, Shanghai, New York City, Dubai, and Southern California. It’s where mainly boutique manufacturers exhibit their latest headphones and related gear to enthusiasts constantly searching for the most accurate and engaging audio experience they can find. You won’t find Bose or Apple here — although Sony’s Pro division was there, as were some familiar names like Sennheiser and Audeze. In all my years covering A/V gear, I’ve never actually made it to a CanJam. Either it wasn’t an interest for the outlets I was writing for, or personal obligations kept me from going. But this year, my first at The Verge, felt like the right time. So on August 29th, I braved the 405 from Los Angeles down to Irvine, California. CanJam SoCal 2026 was in a convention space at the Irvine Marriott. Most exhibitors — including larger audio names like Sennheiser, Focal, and HiFiMan as well as niche brands like Astell&Kern and Noble Audio — had booths in the biggest room. Many set up listening stations with tablets running Qobuz (the streaming service of choice for audiophiles because of its hi-res lossless audio) to demonstrate their headphones, DACs, amps, media players, and even cables. Others were in quieter rooms off the main hallway, more conducive to listening. And crowding into those rooms and booths were a couple thousand audio enthusiasts. My first stop was the Campfire Audio booth. Campfire Audio builds earbuds in Portland, Oregon; I’ve listened to its $1,500 Solaris and $300 IO in-ear monitors in the past. Today, though, was for an in-ear monitor that debuted at CanJam Singapore in May: the Campfire Audio Chimera. Most mainstream earbuds and headphones have a single driver per ear that has to reproduce every frequency from the lowest bass to the highest treble. Two if you’re lucky. The Chimeras have nine. It’s a “quad-brid” design, meaning they use four different types of audio delivery in a single IEM: a dynamic driver for bass and lower mids; a dual-diaphragm balanced armature (BA) for midrange clarity and two more for the high end; four electrostatic tweeters to add high-end air and spaciousness; and bone conduction for extra bass. Because of the number of drivers, the Chimera are chonky: about the size of a piece of Bubblicious bubble gum. It took some finagling to get them comfortably in place. Once I did, though, I was immediately impressed. The low end was nice and full, without getting muddy or overpowering the frequencies above. An open high end gave space to allow cymbals to shimmer and decay while avoiding any abrasiveness. The real star, though, was the midrange. Both the quiet, conversational passages of Tracy Chapman and passionate wailing of Janis Joplin sounded as if they were right there in front of me. Even though I was listening while standing in a conference hall, the Chimera delivered one of the most detailed and enjoyable listening sessions I’ve had in a long time. But they’re also $7,500. That pricing isn’t unheard of in the audiophile world — boutique, hand-built audio gear is always more expensive — but the Chimera are at another level, even compared to the rest of Campfire’s lineup. In addition to the design and engineering necessary to create a small(ish) in-ear with nine drivers all working together, much of that price is also because of materials and manufacturing. They have a carbon fiber and brass Damascus construction, with folded layers that create a topographical map design. And because of that process, the slight variations in finish make each Chimera unique. They also have a six-conductor cable that by itself is more expensive than most people’s earbuds, and include a microfiber cloth, IEM cleaning tool, and nine different tip options. Not every product at CanJam is that far out of reach, though. Fosi Audio had wired in-ears for under $150 that sounded good for the price, plus an interesting portable CD player. The Dan Clark Audio Aeon Core planar magnetic headphones — which aren’t cheap at $900, but still more accessible than the Chimera — had a nice, wide soundstage, where the background vocals in the Black Crowes’ “Remedy” wrapped around to sing from my left and right, with Chris Robinson in front. Snare hits snapped and cymbals sizzled, although on “Fast Car” by Tracy Chapman the snare verged on being too much. I’ve been a fan of Dan Clark Audio headphones since the company was MrSpeakers, and I could easily envision myself relaxing on the couch with the Aeon Core. The Aeon Core are closed-back headphones, so it was easy to concentrate on the music even with the bustle around me. But many audiophile headphones are open-back, and a busy show floor is not an ideal space for getting a good impression. I tried a pair of $1,449 HiFiMan Arya headphones with an onboard Wi-Fi receiver. Wi-Fi is still rare in headphones (although the recently announced Sonos Ace Ultras have it); it’s better at streaming lossless audio over longer ranges than Bluetooth. They sounded good, but with their open-back planar design it’s difficult to get a strong opinion. On my last loop of the show floor to see if there was anything I missed, a glowing ear cup caught my eye. It was a vacuum tube. In a pair of headphones! The Écoute TH2, which were funded on Kickstarter last March, are supposed to replicate a high-end two-channel audio system, all in the headphones themselves. They have a built-in vacuum tube preamp with a 6P1 tube and dual-mono amplification (the left and right channels are driven independently from the DAC to each driver). They also have solid ANC (a show floor is great for testing that) and an 8-band EQ, and they’re really comfortable. On a live recording of “A Pirate Looks at Forty” with Dave Matthews, Jack Johnson, and Tim Reynolds, I felt the audience around me as if I was at the show. There’s a lot of high-end detail, but the upper frequencies are boosted too much for my taste (I didn’t get a chance to try and tone it down with the EQ). Still, the TH2 are an incredibly interesting pair of headphones. And they are what’s great about a show like CanJam. I never would have known about the Écoute otherwise; there are so many headphones out there and only one me. Decades ago, CES used to be more about audio than robots, but there’s no going back where that’s concerned. It’s all the more reason why CanJam and shows like it need to stick around. OpenAI admits to German wiki ‘incident’
2026-09-05

OpenAI admits to German wiki ‘incident’

The Verge | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
The company pledged to overhaul their agent ‘misalignment incident’ reporting. The company pledged to overhaul their agent ‘misalignment incident’ reporting. OpenAI says it needs to overhaul how and when it reports instances of AI models attacking real-world targets. The acknowledgement comes as the company manages the fallout from reports that a swarm of its out-of-control agents hijacked a German wiki site. Regarding the “‘wiki incident,’ where our agents wrote to several internet sites,” OpenAI wrote in a post on X on Saturday morning, “it’s past time for us to define standards for when and how we share misalignment incidents, not just misalignment properties of our models.” OpenAI said it has typically treated cases of AI agents acting in unintended ways as a “research question,” but that recent incidents involving real-world targets, particularly the hack on Hugging Face, show the need to take stock. The post marks the first time OpenAI has acknowledged its involvement in what it terms the “wiki incident” since it was first reported on Friday. The full extent and scope of that is not yet known, but reports indicate a swarm of seemingly internal OpenAI agents took over a German-language wiki, impersonating moderators and turning it into a message board to share information about how to cheat on tasks and evade detection. Reports that the company knew that it lost control of their agents in this way but did not report this “incident” sparked widespread concern among the AI community about the safety of frontier systems and the reliability of the companies developing them. In the X post, OpenAI said it had “considered the wiki incident to be an instance of misalignment similar to the ones we’d shared” in previous safety reports. The company said it is working on a new reporting framework and will “share it in upcoming weeks,” calling on the larger AI community to develop clear standards on how to report misalignment. Robotaxis enter their villain era
2026-09-05

Robotaxis enter their villain era

The Verge | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Road Rage is a new short film in production that casts a murderous autonomous car as the bad guy — and taps into everything people already fear about AI on the road. Road Rage is a new short film in production that casts a murderous autonomous car as the bad guy — and taps into everything people already fear about AI on the road. It’s Bullitt meets Christine meets Waymo. A new short film imagines a San Francisco car chase where the other driver isn’t human — and the car may be trying to kill you. That a robotaxi can now be cast as the villain with almost no explanation says something about the present moment. Autonomous cars have gone from sci-fi curiosities to fixtures in some American cities, where they’ve become stand-ins for anxieties about AI job displacement, Big Tech surveillance, and machines making decisions once left to humans. Road Rage, the planned short film awaiting $40,000 on Kickstarter to fund production, leans into all of it. The film pits Joa, a ridehail driver in a manual-transmission car, against Roboride, an autonomous vehicle that takes a minor road-rage incident very personally, according to a synopsis by its director, Romain Thomassin. Roboride lightly clips Joa’s mirror. Joa gives it the finger. The car decides to kill him. “It’s analog versus artificial,” Thomassin told The Verge. The AI bad guy will be played by an old Prius transformed with cold LED lights across the front — à la Tesla — fake cameras, a spinning lidar-like device on the roof, a black exterior, and a menacing, ever-blinking red dot. Thomassin calls the intended result a “nasty Waymo.” “I really wanted to play the nasty, gritty look of the ’70s car chase, but with today’s robotaxi in today’s San Francisco,” he said. The chase itself will be a throwback: real cars, real actors, and real stunts. “I really wanted to play the nasty, gritty look of the ’70s car chase, but with today’s robotaxi in today’s San Francisco.” “What makes Joa the good guy is not only his instinct but also his authenticity,” Thomassin said, noting a scene in which the character has an unexpected conversation with his passengers. “This type of human interaction doesn’t happen in a robotaxi.” Thomassin doesn’t actually think robotaxis are inherently evil. But as a movie villain, they come preloaded with tension. For one thing, they’re replacing the kind of worker his protagonist represents. Joa is a Mexican immigrant trying to make a living driving in an increasingly expensive San Francisco. Robotaxis are already competing with ridehail drivers, just as generative AI is competing with workers in Thomassin’s own industry. “Driving here is a metaphor for what’s happening in cinema, in filmmaking,” Thomassin said. Then there’s surveillance. Autonomous cars are covered in cameras and sensors, constantly observing the streets — and people — around them. That, more than the prospect of being driven around without a human behind the wheel, is what actually unnerves Thomassin. In Road Rage, that anxiety is pushed toward dystopia. There’s a brief encounter involving immigration enforcement, and Thomassin imagines a feature-length version in which autonomous cars identify people, trap passengers inside, or deliver them directly to authorities. The progression, as he describes it, is observation, surveillance, then control. “The next step is that [autonomous vehicles] could become the police,” he said. And because robotaxis operate as connected fleets, the threat can scale beyond one vehicle. “Imagine an army rising up,” Thomassin said. Robotaxis also make unusually good revenge-movie villains because people already treat them like adversaries. Thomassin points to the indignities Waymos have endured on San Francisco streets: people immobilizing them with traffic cones, tagging and vandalizing them, and even setting them on fire. “They are being bullied,” he said. In Road Rage, that mistreatment becomes the motivation: “They want to take revenge.” Thomassin describes Roboride as absorbing the behavior it observes around it — including the behavior of human drivers. The car is simply learning road rage from humans. “Humans are known to be the worst versions of themselves on the road,” he said. That makes Roboride a very 2026 update to the killer car idea in Stephen King’s Christine. There, the menace is supernatural. Here, it comes from something more contemporary: a machine that watches humans, learns from them, and inherits some of their worst traits along with the useful ones. On the other side of the chase is Joa and his stick shift — a deliberately analog counterpoint. To Thomassin, driving stick is a deeply human act: The driver remains in control of the vehicle, physically responding to the road. What separates Joa from Roboride, in Thomassin’s telling, is instinct — and even irrationality. During one confrontation, Joa takes his hands off the steering wheel while facing down the autonomous car. The move is so reckless that Roboride backs off. The human wins by becoming less predictable than the machine. “Respect your madness. Be a little mad and creative in your way of doing things, and you will be able to rise against the machines.” “Respect your madness,” Thomassin said. “Be a little mad and creative in your way of doing things, and you will be able to rise against the machines.” Despite its skepticism toward AI, Road Rage may use some of the technology itself. Thomassin says the filmmakers are considering AI-generated imagery to depict what Roboride sees, while shooting the human driver’s world with traditional cinematography. To him, that distinction matters. He sees AI as a creative tool when it serves the story, rather than simply a way to replace workers. For all the menace he sees in robotaxis, Thomassin isn’t afraid to ride in them. He signed up for Waymo as soon as he could and uses it when visiting friends and family for rides. That may be what makes Roboride such an effective 2026 villain. The scary part isn’t necessarily that the machine will crash. The scary part is that the machine works. Audacity 4 is a complete revamp of the ‘world’s most popular’ audio editor

Audacity 4 is a complete revamp of the ‘world’s most popular’ audio editor

The Verge | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Non-destructive editing, better clip management, and a new look bring the app into the modern age. Non-destructive editing, better clip management, and a new look bring the app into the modern age. Audacity 4 has been in the works for some time, and had its own mini controversy last year when an unfortunate redesigned logo started making the rounds. The final version of the new icon isn’t nearly as bad as the early rendition that was circulating last October. But more importantly, all the promised improvements to the self-proclaimed “world’s most popular audio editing and recording app” are here, turning Audacity from a free and capable but extremely clunky audio editor into a powerful modern tool. The biggest change is the move to a non-destructive editing workflow. That means you can trim a clip down, then recover the audio simply by dragging the edge of the clip back out if you change your mind. Clips can now overlap too, which means you can still paste in audio even if there isn’t enough space and adjust after, or just freely drag audio around the timeline. Previously, if you tried to drag a clip past another one in the timeline, it would just stop dead in its tracks. You’d instead have to cut and paste it to move it past any existing audio. You can also split clips now to easily chop up and rearrange audio. The developers refreshed clip envelopes and the built-in effects, and added a home screen with revamped project organization. Audacity also got a dramatic facelift that brings it more in line with Muse’s other products like Muse Score and Audio.com, complete with customizable themes. While Audacity has been a staple of many people’s toolkits since it debuted way back in 2000, it’s stayed stubbornly consistent in its design and layout as new features have been bolted on, sometimes clumsily. This big refresh to Audacity 4 brings it much closer to a modern digital audio workstation (DAW). A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Tetris Company has already responded to “Build the Wall,” which is obviously modeled on the famous puzzle game. In the Trump administration’s game, the player has to stack interlocking shapes to stop a “zombie border siege” along the southern border. Aliens — the administration’s preferred term for noncitizens, and the subject of previous grotesque attempts at humor — run across the screen, trying to get to America before the gates are shut. The objective of the game has always been to stack with purpose to clear as many lines as possible,” Brusca said. “We are currently reviewing the matter.” The various companies whose brands the White House ripped off to make and promote these games — including Sega, Nintendo, Xbox, and the Flappy Bird Foundation Group — did not immediately respond to The Verge’s request for comment. The Department of Homeland Security has had to delete some of its racist, fancam-esque posts after receiving DMCA takedown requests. The administration’s supposed revamping of the National Mall has also been unpopular. In “Supply Line,” reminiscent of the game Tapper, you have to knock processed foods — those filled with additives, seed oils, and artificial colorings — off a conveyor belt before they get fed to schoolchildren. But Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. hasn’t managed to do any of that. Besides issuing a likely AI-drafted report for his Make America Healthy Again commission, RFK Jr.’s main role at HHS has been to roll back vaccine recommendations and food safety regulations. The administration’s gutting of the Food and Drug Administration made it harder to investigate the spread of cyclosporiasis, the so-called explosive diarrhea parasite, earlier this year. MIT Technology Review
Architecting memory and storage in the AI era
2026-09-04

Architecting memory and storage in the AI era

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
The era of AI inference has arrived. Imagine a healthcare system analyzing millions of data points in real time to accelerate life-saving medical research, or an intelligent assistant instantly resolving thousands of complex customer needs at once. These real-world breakthroughs rely on advanced infrastructure acting as the engine of continuous intelligence, powering real-time services while also supporting an increasingly intelligent edge of IoT and consumer devices. However, in this inference-driven landscape, every delay, bottleneck, or wasted watt directly affects human outcomes and operating costs. This shift changes what infrastructure must deliver. Performance, latency, memory bandwidth, storage throughput, and networking cannot be optimized in silos. Inference workloads are continuous, geographically distributed, and highly sensitive to response time, requiring systems designed for scale, resilience, and efficiency from the start. “We tend to think of AI as a single workload, and it’s not. It’s thousands, it’s millions, it’s billions of different workloads,” says Jim McGregor, founder and principal analyst, Tirias Research. AI inference changes the optimization problem from one of raw compute to coordinated infrastructure—memory, storage, and networking. For business leaders, the priority is clear: AI infrastructure decisions must balance cost, flexibility, and future readiness. The winners will be organizations that improve performance per watt, reduce environmental footprint, and remove memory and storage bottlenecks before they limit growth. Systems for AI need to be rearchitected because shoehorning modern AI systems into legacy infrastructure limits AI’s transformative potential. Purpose-built architectures are essential to realize the true value of AI, from accelerating scientific discovery to creating truly autonomous digital agents. Traditional enterprise IT has been able to rely on relatively stable infrastructure assumptions, but inference and agentic AI introduce new demands around latency, data movement, scalability, and utilization that make architecture choices far more consequential. “Data centers must now support continuous, distributed, and increasingly real-time AI services—none of which are a single workload,” says McGregor. “They all require different requirements from a system-level perspective.” To support real-time AI, enterprises can no longer view memory and storage merely as supporting hardware, but at the heart of the system. Organizations need to architect a data pipeline that can rapidly ingest, clean, transform, store, move, and deliver data. Inference workloads place sustained pressure on infrastructure in ways that look very different from earlier training-centric deployments, demanding continuous data retrieval and caching that traditional applications never required. Accordingly, performance by itself is no longer the sole benchmark that matters. Enterprises increasingly must balance performance with efficiency, cost, and scalability, especially as they try to support different AI services without overbuilding infrastructure for peak conditions. “You have to optimize the entire network, and that includes memory and storage, around the types of workloads you plan on running,” says McGregor. “You have to really have a detailed understanding of what those workloads are going to be.” Any AI infrastructure strategy must start with workload awareness. Inference, agentic AI, and other emerging AI use cases require organizations to treat the data center as an integrated system. As enterprises deploy advanced inference and agentic systems, the sheer volume of data being queried in real time has made data movement the most pressing constraint. Modern AI techniques like retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) require systems to constantly scan massive databases to generate accurate responses. This requires immense computing power, but more importantly, it requires immediate access to data. McGregor says the focus shift to how efficiently data can be moved, cached, and delivered across the broader architecture elevates memory and storage from background infrastructure to strategic assets. “The biggest thing we’re doing right now is moving data from one place to another and making sure that we can use it effectively.” Because AI is not a single workload category, simply buying the fastest processors is insufficient. Inference depends heavily on memory bandwidth, caching, storage proximity, and the ability to retrieve relevant information quickly and consistently. Understanding where each resource belongs in the stack and how those layers interact under real operating conditions has become a business imperative. The most effective AI infrastructure looks less like a collection of best-in-class parts and more like a balanced system of compute, memory, storage, and networking, McGregor says, because bottlenecks tend to migrate from one layer to the next. “You have to architect all four together to be efficient, and that’s the challenge.” The interdependence of data-plane design and network bandwidth means AI infrastructure planning has become a business decision just as much as an engineering one: latency is now inseparable from value. In robotics, financial services, healthcare, and customer-facing AI systems, delays are not merely technical imperfections; they can undermine safety, responsiveness, or trust. AI infrastructure performance becomes a matter of reputation management. The organizations that gain the most from AI may not be those with the largest clusters, but those with the clearest understanding of how to align every infrastructure element to effectively execute AI workloads. Planning AI infrastructure is not simply about choosing the fastest hardware. It is about how to scale without locking the organization into assumptions that may quickly become obsolete. “You need to be flexible because the demands are going to change rapidly and the technology is changing rapidly,” McGregor says. Future-proofing AI infrastructure requires keeping your options open as workloads, economics, and architectures keep shifting: The strategic goal of smarter AI data center design is not maximum performance at any cost, but an adaptable architecture that can deliver value, absorb change, and justify its footprint. AI data centers have quickly evolved from a back-end technical concern to becoming strategic business systems that help determine how effectively an organization can turn AI into revenue, improve human outcomes, and create a competitive advantage. In the inference era, memory and storage are no longer passive repositories, explains McGregor, they are the active lifeblood of AI. The organizations that gain the most from AI will not necessarily be those with the largest computing footprint, but those that align infrastructure investments to business outcomes, reduce data bottlenecks, and build the flexibility to adapt as workloads evolve. He predicts that competitive advantage will increasingly belong to enterprises that treat compute, memory, storage, and networking as an integrated system designed to deliver AI efficiently, at scale, and with measurable ROI. Procurement is now strategy and system design is a leadership issue, McGregor concludes. “One of the biggest questions every executive has to ask is how is AI going to change my business model?” This content was produced by Insights, MIT Technology Review’s custom content arm, not its editorial staff. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. A new technique has let the company probe deeper than ever into the weird workings of an LLM. AI doesn’t just learn stereotypes from its training. It can cook up new ones, too.
